PRESS RELEASE
21-December 1945
LAST OF THE SUMLARIES OF INDUSTRIAL INVENTORIES
The following are summarics of the last of twenty-three separate
inventories on Japanese industry prepared and completed on Dec. 1 by
the Industrial and Research Divisions of the Economic and Scientific
Section, SCAP, for Ambassador Edwin W. Pauley, chief of the United
States reparations mission in Japan. The inventories are based upon
statistics and statements of the Japanese government and industry
cuthorities.

AUTOMOTIVE
Standard Passenger dars
Neither Toyota or Nissan, the two Japanese con panies thet manu-
factured strndarc size passenger cars, have produced any new cars
since 1944 because of government restrictions. In 1943, Toyota pro-
duced 66 and Nissan 291, compared with 458 by Toyota anc 1,211 by Nis-
san in 1938. During 1941, 1942 and 1943, the two firms producec a
tot 1 of 2,193.
The facilities of these corpanies have been largely used for
truck ranufacture so that ct present neither is prep red to manufac-
ture passenger cars. However, when the necessary changes are made,
Toyota will be able to manufecture about 150 cars a year (in addition
to the present ye rly output of 15,000 trucks) and Nissan  bout 500
cars a year (in addition to 15,coo trucks also).
Midget Passenger Cars
The principal manufacturer of midget (Datsun) passenger cars has
been the Nisson Company. There were a fer other manufacturers in the
field before the var but production was limited and it was abandoned
entirely when the war started.
No production of micgct cars at all w's reported in 1944. The
last reported year was 1943 when 233 midgets were manufactured, com-
pared with 511 in 1941, 773 in 1939 end 2,789 in 1938. Manufacture
has been lirite: in recent yerrs to filling military orders only.
The company reported it is preparec to restore production but may re-
quire sone time to do it.
Light Trucks
The Nissan Company, manufacturer of the midget passenger car,
likewise is the principal prolucer of Japan's light four-wheeled truck.
Zero production wos reporte: for 1944, conpared with 204 in 1943, 928
in 1941, 2,178 in 193 d 4,224 in 1938. The company plans to resume
work as soon as circum  nces permit.
There are six nanufacturers of the light three-wheeled truck in
Japan but their present production capacities are unknown. Material
shortages and air raic ctmage affected production seriously. The only
'ata available showed production of 13,831 in 1938, 15,172 in 1937
and 11,167 in 1936.
Heavy Trucks
Nissan, Toyota and the Diesel Compony ere the tree principal pro-
ducers of heavy trucks. Toyota is now producing about 5oo a month
and expects to be turning out 1,000 a month early in 1946. Nissan,
in worse condition than Toyota, is producing less than loo a month.
Diesel had a pre-war capacity of 8,o0o units a year butsits 1944 pro-
duction was only 3,845, much 1ess in 1945.
Toyota reached peck truck production in 1941 nd 1942 with
15,555 and 15,558 in the two years respectively but fell off to 9,769
n 1943, rising slightly to 10,689 in 1944. Nissan's truck production,
t 8,299 in 1938, hit iss peak in 1941 with 17,276 but dropped to
6,181 in 1942, 9,724 in 1943 and 7,012 in 1944.
The pre-war truck production capacity of the three companies was
.6,000 but during the war, the cutomobile industry suffered from
os cf meteri.

shortages of materials and loss of workers due to the pressure of
more important or urgent demands such as those of the aircraft indus-
try. Many sub-contractors in the industry turned to more profitable
businesses, adding to the difficulties of the cutomobile makers.
The three manufacturers face a number of serious difficulties
in re-commencing manufacture on a commercial scale. Their problems
include shortages of housing, food, clothing and transportation for
the workers as well as critical shortages of raw materials.

NO. 17
NON--FERROUS METALS
Copper
Copper production occupied an important part in Japan's metal
industries, particularly in manufacture of munitions. It started
early in Japan, although production in 1876 was less than 4 million
kilograms and half of that was exported. Production, however, grew
rapidly, reaching 78,614,000 kilograms in 1936. But consumption
likewise increased repidly so that it was necessary to import 47,794,
000 kilograms in 1936.
It was during the years 1931-1936 that Japan changed from an
xport to an import nation for copper. In 1931 imports were only
2.019,900 kilograms against exports of 26,603,200 kilograms and
omestic consumption of 51,264,881. By 1934 imports had risen to
i.368,300 kilograms against exports of only 12,621,600 kilograms
hile domestic consumption had risen to 105,748,970. In 1936, with
:mports of 47,794,000 kilograms, exports totaled only 7,000 kilograns
md demestic consumption was 127,524,000 kilograms.
During the war years, however, Japan was practically cut off
from her principal import market nd had to depend on local produe-
tion, her colonies and reclaimed copper. Yet Japan was stili able
t., produce 123,721,000 kilograms of electrolytic copper in 1943,
producing 68,881,00o of it from concentrates from Japen proper,
3,816,000 from Korea, 4,027,000 from Formosa and 5,789,000 from other
foreign districts. Scrap accounted for another 26,427,000 kilograms;
dump and residue, 4,763,000; scrap from munitions, 9,671,000, and
miscellaneous, 347,O00. Sourccs for 1944 production totaling
99,196,000 kilograms were: Concentrates from Japan proper, 54,975,000;
from Korea, 4,475,000; from Formosa, 3,281,000; from other foreign
districts, 3,203,000; scrap, 19,334,000; dump and rcsidue, 3,425,000;
scrap from munitions, 8,202,000; miscelianeous, 2,301,000.
In 1943, Japan's copper requirements were 75,000,000 kilograms
(50 per cent) for ammunition, 30,o00,000 (20 per cent) for power
transmission and communications, and 15,O00,o0o (10 per cent) apiece
for (1) marine uses, (2) air nd land transport and (3) other in-
dustrial uses.
The production of cable nd wire is controlled by six large
companies while three companies operete the main copper rolling mills.
Japan currently can produce between 60,000,000 and 70,000,000
kilograms of refined copper in the home islands, and this amount was
believed probably to be about equivalent to her peacetime needs. The
smelting and refining industries are developed to a capacity far
above that which can be met with domestic ores and much in excess of
peace-time requirements for copper.
Actual production of copper concentrate ore from Japanese mines
during 1940-1944 (in kilograms): 1940, 68,321,000; 1941, 69,900,000;
1942, 78,110,000; 1943, 92,131,000; 1944,78,463,000.
During 1943 and 1944, eleven mi es operated by three companies
produced approximately 50 per cent of the copper concentrate mined
in Japan. 0f the 1943 production totaling 43,846,000 kilograms,
five Mitsubishi Mining Company mines produced 19,721,O00; three
Nippon Mining Company mines, 16,096,000; three Furukawa Mining
Company mines, 11,029,000. Of the 1944 production totaling
78,463,000, the figures were; Mitsubishi, 14,954,000; Nippon Mining,

Zinc
Records for the war period in Japan seem to indicate that mine
production excceded smelter capacity. A possible explanation of
this is that lead nd zinc occur together, and that the urgent demand
for lead ore forced the production of more zinc than the smelters
could treat.
Zinc mine production records for the years prior to 1940 were
not available but for the subsequent years production was as follows
(in kilograms of zinc content): 1940, 43,972,000; 1941, 52,924,000;
1942, 84,864,000; 1943, 93,307,000; 1944, 75,870. During the same
period, zinc smelter production was: 1940, 59,703,000 kilograms;
1941,61,092,000; 1942, 60,196,000; 1943,62,280,000; 1944,
61,200,0ć0.
Imports of zinc metal between 1925 and 1940 inclusive ranged
from a 1ow of 23,915,000 kilograms in 1925 to a peak of 58,220,000
in 1939. During the four years 194l-1944, however, imports for the
entire period totaled only 13,840,000 kilograms.
Zine ore imports during the three-year period 1942-1944 totaled
only 19,680,000 kilograms of which 9,962,000 came from Manchukuo,
9,105,000 from Korea, 381,000 from Burma and 232,000 from French
Indo-China.

Lead
Japanese lead production (in kilograms of refined lead) rose
from 3,337,000 in 1925 to 7,807,000 in 1935, 22,655,000 in 1940,
25,716,000 in 1941, 25,832,000 in 1942, 32,031,000 in 1943, and
34,929,000 in 1944
During the seven years 1935-194l inclusive, metallic lead im-
ports totaled 577,042,000 kilograms of which 302,201,000 (about 52
per cent) came from the United States and Canada. A part of the
balance was accounted for with 33,414,000 from India in 1935-1936
and 71,342,000 from Mexico and 33,453,000 from Burma during 1939-1941.
Practically all imports during 1942-1944, totaling 6,489,000 kilo-
grams, came from Manchuria (4,999,000) and China (1,398,000 in 1944).
Lead ore imports during the war came mostly from Manchukuo.
During 1942-1943, imports totaled 4,847,000 kilograms of which Man-
chukuo provided 4,431,000.
The Mitsubishi and Mitsui Mining Companies dominated the lead
mining field, the two of them producing 9,894,000 kilograms of Japan's
total production in 1944 of 17,344,C00 kilograms.
Three lead smelters with a 1945 production caprcity of
37,380,000 kilograms reportcd actua1 production of 20,167,000 in 1944,
21,236,000 in 1943, 16,489,000 in 1942, 15,507,000 in 1941 and
13,727,000 in 1940.
Five refineries with a 1945 production capacity of 53,800,000
kilograms produced 34,929,000 in 1914, 31,494,000 in 1943, 23,213,000
in 1942, 25,716,C00 in 1941, 22,655,000 in 1940 and 7,807,000 in 1935.
One refinery at Hiroshima, with a listcd capacity of 15,000,000 kil-
ograms in 1944, did not begin operating until 1943. What damage it
sustained was not noted.
TIN
Tin refinery production rose from 392,000 kilograms in 1925 to
930,000 in 1930 and 2,095,000 in 1935 before it declined gradually
to 1,398,000 in 1939, bui1ding up again to a peak of 3,816,000 in
1942, and dropping once more to 1,815,000 in 1943 and 772,000 in 1944.
Practically all such production was carried on at one refinery through
1941, when a few smaller ones were built and put into production.
Imports of refined tin were between 3,000,000 and 4,500,000
kilograms annually from 1925 to 1937. They rose to 8,744,000 in
1938, 7,063,000 1n 1939, 10,869,000 in 1940, 5,480,000 in 1941
9,706,000 in 1943 and 11,227,000 in 1944. There was no import data

for.1942. During 1935-1944 (minus 1942), refined tin imports totaled

tlements and 13,821 from the Dutch Indies. During 1943 and 1944
alone, imports from the Straits were over 14,0c0,000 kilograms.
The mine production of tin came in large part from the Mitsubishi
mine at Minamatani and the Toyo Mining Company mine at Iwate. Of

Imports of tin concentrates fell from 1,564,000 kilograms in

The main tin smelting plant in Japan is the Ikuno smelter at
Hyogo. It has a production capacity of 6,O0o,O0o kilograms annually
but its actual production in 1943 was only 2,448,000.
Stocks of metallic tin in Japan as of 17 November 1945 totaled
4,953,000 kilograms.

HANDICRAFT INDUSTRY
In 1939, Japan exported nearly 107 million yen worth of handi-
craft industry wares, including 46 million worth of ceramics, 22 mil-
lion in cultured pearls, 21 million in,glassware, 11 million in jew-
elyy, 3 million in bomboo wares, and 1 million in lacquer ware.
A survey has shown that the industry can produce an estirated
100,000,000 yen worth of such goods for export in 1946, including
30 million worth of toys, 27 million in textile handicraft goods
12 rillion in brmboo and wood, 1† rillion in curios and objets dlart,
700,000 in lacquer ware, and bout 500,000 apiece in cloisenne, jew-
elry nd pictures n carvings. Also 5,00o,000 in porcelain.
CERAMICS: Principal factories are located prirarily in Nagoya,
Yokkaichi and Gifu prefecture. Fifty per cent of the industry was
destroyed at Yokkaichi an sore in Gifu have been converted to muni-
tions. Darage at Nagoye 's slight. It is estinnted that six to
twelve ronths will be required to put all factories back into produc-
tion. Available for inrediate export is 5,149,745 yen and expected
pro ucticn in 1946 is cnother 5 rillion.
LACQUER WARE: Future prccuction is extinate: at 70o,C00 yen in
1946 and 1,000,000 yen in 1947, compared with 46 million yen in 1938.
At present the incustry is at a standstill. Manufacture of lacquer
fcll off when naturai inc artificial lacquer juice, an inport from

ToYs: There are 170 monufacturers and 100 wholesalers in the toy
industriel center at Osake. They are capable, with present materials,
of turning out 3, o0o. oo yen worth of toy with proluction in 1946
estimeted at 30 million yen.
CULTURED PEARLS: In 194O the pear1 industry wos placec under gov-
ernment restrictions and 250 out of the 360 pear1 cultivators in Japan
werc forced out of business. Prior to this the normal yield was 30
million rother of pearls cnnually. Cultivetion of pearls was fortid-
den when var broke out in 194!, The existing pearl stock is therefore
of pre-war production and is too srall in quantity to be of value for

CLOISENNE: It is estimated that 500,O00 yen worth of cloisenne
can be produced if operation can start immediately. There were about
50 skilled artisans in the trade before the war but many of them have
entered other fields of work since then.
BAMB00: Bamboo fishing rods exported curing 1937-1939 had a total
value of 4ll,Ooo yen and the making of these can stert irmediately.
Additional bamboo articles with an export value of 12,200,000 yen can

PAPER LANTERNS: Annual productoon formerly ha: a value of 1,100,000
yen. However, eight of the ten factories for them have been Cestroyed.
Child labor is used in rost lantern factories.
GLASSWARE: Jewelry and glass ornaments for export formerly had a
value of two million yen annually, and other glass produced had an ex-
port value of 25 million yen a year. Present production capacity is
estimated at 200,000 tons a year, mainly in bottles,bulbs, medical
tubes, table ware, optical glass, etc.

BUILDING MATERIALS
PLASTER: Plaster is not factory-prepared in Japan but is mixed
on the job by the workmen from lime, clay and water and applied over
a bamboo lattice to form walls. Approximately 1,O0o smali factories
produce lime anc total procuction in 1943 was 1,800,000 metric tons.
Present production is reported substantially the same. Plaster is
also made from gypsum of which approximately 130,000 metric tons is
produced annually in Japan. Japan, beginning in 1937, launched an
effort to becore self-sufficient for gypsum but home proluction was
far short of consumption and it is considered probable she will have
to irport a substantial part of her post-war needs.
GLASS: There are five plants with an annual capacity of 1,800,000
cases (45 kilograms per case) of flat glass, and approximately 150
with a capacity of 20o,ooo metric tons of blown or moulded glass.
Current shortages of coal and soda ash are greatly reducing glass
production, and importation of soda ash was terned essential if pro-
duction in quantity is to be continued. Before 194l, there were
1,O8o glass works in Japan, not including plate or optical glass.
Most of them were handwork except for a few cutomatic bottle machines,
The number was recuced to about 18o in accordance with government
regulations in 1941 and 1943. Danaged in air raids were 68, but
about 150 are still operating.
CEMENT: Approximately 35 plants in Japan are procucing cement.
Production in 1944 was 2,959,686 metric tons, including 1,925,152
metric tons of Portland cement. This compared vith production total-
ing 6,002,379 metric tons in 1940, including 4,401,601 metric tons
of Portland cement. Production capacity dropped fron 7,603,O00 metric
tons in 1940 to 4,490,000 in 1944. As of 1944, it was estimated
that production capacity was approximately 1,3ó8,0oo metric tons in
excess of Japanese emands for cement. Estimated exports (no cement
is imported) were 973.233 metric tons in 1940, 692,349 in 1941,
168,330 in 1942, 126,023 in 1943, 31,530 in 1944 and none in 1945.
SAND AND GRAVEL: Sand and gravel is widely distributed and is
avilable in almost unlimiied quantities throughout Japen. However,
the industry is not organize? to the extent that reliable production
data is recorded. Very little machinery is erployed; small dredges
are used to obtain and wash river gravels, end hand labor alone is
used to work bank gravel deposits.
LIME: There are approximately 685 lire factories in Japan with
an annual production capacity of 1,649,000 metric tons. Actual pro-
duction was 1,683,634 metric tons in 1940, 2,37,172 in 1941.
2,467,511 in 1942, 1,775,231 in 1943, 992,838 in 1944 and 326,110
for the first half of 1945. For the quarter ending 30 June 1945,
consumption in Japan was 150,560 metric tons of which 65,750 went
to agriculture, 44,820 to the chemica1 industry, 25,800 to refractory
purposes, and 14,190 to the building industry.

INDUSTRIAL INVENTORY
NO. 20
MACHINERY
(OTHER THAN MACHINE TOOLS)
The records of the Production Machinery Control Association
were burned as a result of the May 25th air raid so that statisti-
cal data obtained from then is at best an estimate only and useful
for little but checking purposes. During the war, because of direct
Army and Navy purchases, the associntion controlled only about 35
per cent of the production in its field. No worthwhile source of
information regarding Army and Navy controlled productive capacity
has been foun? yet.
Compilation of accurate statistics ceased at the end of 1942
and nuch of the production during the war was a closely guarced Army
and Navy secret so that no civilian agency is in possession of the
information. The Commerce and Industry Ministry has plotted graphs
indicating, fron accurate data, the trend of production capacity to
the end of 1942. A projection of these curves, modified by factors
that are known--such as war damage--is the basis on which the fig-
ures in this inventory are given.
All figures are said by the Japanese to include all capacity
such as Army, Navy, Government railways, etc., as well as normal
civilian producers. The figures for numbers of factories include
only the principa1 makers but account for over 90 per cent of the
total capacity. In Japanese industry, the large manufacturers pro-
duce, in the same factories, such wicely different items as steam
turbines an? gasoline engines so that statistics as to nur:ber of
factories will include the same factory under several different cat-
egories. However, since the total number of large producers is
small, the percentage of error in total number of factories is not
great.
INTERNAL COLBUSTION ENGINES: There are a total of 4O factories
for making Diesel, gas, gasoline and oil engines. Their annual
capacity is 8,000 metric tons, plus another 56,000 metric tons of
marine engines. Th-ra are about 25o other factories furnishing parts
and sub-assemblies  the principal manufacturers.
STEAM BOILERS   CCESSORIES: Twenty-one factories are capable
of turning out 21,000 metric tons annually, plus another 45,500
metric tons of marine boilers. Neither figure includes locomotive
boilers. These figures are lower in reletion to pre-war production
than might be expected. However, emphasis on use of hydro-electric
power as a coal-saving measure, anc conversion of factories to wer
production that formerly made boilers for heating purposes, accounts
for the difference.
WATER WHEELS, TURBINES AND ACCESSORIES: Nine factories have a
total annual production capacity of 3,3o0 metric tons. The figure
includes hydro-power plant accessories as well as the prime movers
themselves. The accessories nake up about 8o per cent of the total,
which is believed to be reasonably accurate.
PUMPS AND COMPRESSORS: Potential production capacity, three to
six months from now, is estimated at 26,000 metric tons a year from
123 factories for water, oil, gas and air pumps and compressors.
Considerable conversion of war industry to this type of production
is planned.
BLOWING MACHINES: The potential production capacity three to
six months from now is estimated at 4,267 metric tons of fans, cen-

CRANES, HOISTS, CONVEYORS, ELFVATORS: There are 118 factories

IRON AND STEEL FORGINGS AND CASTINGS
While Japan imported much iron and steel, it was in the form
of pig iron and screp rather than in shapes such as forgings and
castings. Her exports of iron and steel procucts were finished itens
and not forgings and castings requiring further finishing. Even
after the establishment of large iron and steel industries in Man-
churia, the capacity for forging and casting of commercial shapes
remained in Japan proper.
Production of steel forgings rose from 25,500 metric tons from
12 fectories in 1927 to 26,900 metric tons from 15 factories in 1930
and to 178,500 metric tons from 83 factories in 1944. It is. estimat-
ed that the present cap city is 386,000 metric tons a year from 106
factories.
Steel castings production increased from 43,145 metric tons
from 18 factories in 1927 to 38,661 metric tons from. 23 factories
in 1933 to 280,634 metric tons fron 242 factories in 1944. Present
production capacity is estimated at 350,000 metric tons a year from
257 factories.
Production of iron castings rose from 182,00 metric tons in
1927 to 255,000 in 1928 before slurping to 120,000 in 1930. The
numher of factories for the respective years was 9'7, 1,100 and
1,228. Production in 1944 was 1,130,000 metric tons from 3,605
factories. Present capacity is estimated at 904,O00 metric tons
from 2,884 factories.
Iron castings production (malleable cast iron) was 15,00o metric
tons in 1927 from 40 factories. It increased to 17,000 tons from 45
factories in 1930 and was 48,000 from 125 factories in 1944. Present
capacity is estimated at 38,óoo metric tons from 92 factories. A
small amount was both inported and exported curing 1926-1930. The
imports mainly were automotive parts anc exports were 1 ikewise auto-
motive parts plus bicycles, etc.

OTHER MINING
Gold
In 1942, the last year when Japanese gold mines opcrated at ca-
pacity, there were in the home islands 30 mines producing more than
100 kiiograns each per year. The present production capacity of these
and other mines cannot be gauged accurately from production prior to
1942 since this production wes stimulated by government subsidy, and
also since theeverage grade of ore has dropped about 40 per cent due
to intensive mining prior to 1943.
Under conditions not expested to obtain in gold mining until
autumn of 1946, production is expected to reach a rate of about 9,s00
kilograms a year, including 4,600 on Honshu, 3,500 on Hokkaido, and
1,600 on Kyushu.
Gold mine production in Japan, Korea and Formosa, in terms of
kilograms of go1d content, totaled 602,458 kilograms during the 18
years 1927-1944 inclusive. 0f this amount, 300,533 was mined in
Japan proper, 275,735 in Korea nd 16,190 in Formosa. Production in
Japan proper hit a pe k of 25,926 kilograms in 1929 but fell thereafter
to 25,583 in 1940; 25,328 in 1941; 22,915 in 1942; 11,465 in 1943;
and 5,553 in 1944. Production in Korca reached its penk 29,192i a1so
in 1939, but fe11 afterwards to 25,288 in 1940; 25,584 in 1941; 23,755
in 1942; 16,642 in 1943; nd 7,769 in 1944. Pek production in Formosa
was 1,688 kilograms in 1938, nd 1944 production there was 718 kilo-
grams.
Silver
The principal silver mines in Japan mumber 44. Most of the sil-
ver produced in Japan is a by-product of smelting of copper, lead and
zinc. Of 344,176 kilograms of silver produced by the princip-1 mines
in 1939, a tota1 of 237,100 came from copper mines, 30,572 from 1ead-
zinc mines and 76,504 from mines producing gold and silver only.
Four mines (three copper nd one gold)accounted for 51 per cent of
the silver production in 1939. Most of the ore is of very low grade,
judged by 'American standards. Devclopments in copper, lead nd zinc
smelting will detcrmine the amount of silver to be smelted in the
future.
Silver production rose gradually in Japan from 126,194 kilograms
in 1925 to 175,063 in 1939; 256,007 in 1935, nd the: pea of 436,148
in 1939. Therenfter 1t dropped to 358,410 in 1940; 360,298 in 1941;
328,273 in 1942; 252,289 in 1943; 229,157 in 1944.
Sulphur and Pyrite
Japan has always been seli-suricient in sulphur and pyrite and
exported sulphur to all parts of eastern Asia and thelWestern Pacific
area. The increased demand for sulphuric acid caused by Japan's mil-
itary expansion was met by increased mine production until about 1941.
Since then, mine production of sulphur and pyrite has fallen off near-
ly to pre-wer levels. Nevertheless, her mines cn readily satisfy
all her peace-time needs and probably can produce ar cxportable sur-
plus. Her sulphuric acid capacity is two or three times her pence-
time consumption.
Japan's sulphur production was 61,500 metric tons in 1931, and
it rose rapidly to 144,426 in 1933;164,945 in 1935, anc to a penH of
238,148 in 1937. It dropped thereafter to 205,812 in 1939; 201,333 in
-1-

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
1941; 164,499 in 1942; 147,700 in 1943, and 71,523 in 1944.
Pyrite production was 560,000 metric tons in 1931, rose to
1,338,891 in 1935, and hit a peak of 2,122,000 in 1938. It then
fe11 to 1,519,000 in 1941; 1,338,106 in 1942; 1,167,710 in 1943, and
716,188 1n 1944.
CHROMIUM
Japan's supply of chromite is fairly adequate. Up to 1939 she
was not a heavy importer but during the Philippines occupation, she
used much pressure to mine anc ship Philippines ore. It would appear
that she might maintain, for c few years, a production of bout
40k000 tons from the home islands. However, in recent years, she
has workec ceposits not orcinarily eccnomical. Furthermore, most
chromite deposits are notoriously impersistent and her reserves may
be seriously depleted.
To satisfy Japan's Cemand for bout 15,o00 tons of ferrochrome,
approximately 30,Ooo tons of chromite would be neeced, leaving only
about 20,Ooo tons from domestic sources for refractory nd chemical
uses. Her ferrochrome plant capacity is in excess of that which can
be met by Comestic ores and probably in excess of what is required
by a peacetime steel industry.
Chromite production in Japan annually: 1930, 11,348 metric tons;
1935, 36，309; 1940, 53,550;1941,61,560; 1942, 60,989;1943,
64,280; 1944, 81,481. Imports fluctuated between 1,000 and 5,000
metric tons during 1933-1938 but rose to 13,542 in 1939; 37,300 in
1940; 40,000 in 1942; 50,000 in 1943, and 20,000 in 1944. Prior to
1936 nearly a11 imports came from New Caledonia. From 1939 on, how-
ever, nearly all came from the Philippines.
MANGANESE
Japanese production of manganese rose from only 12,040 metric
tons in 1925 to over 400,000 in 1944. The grade of concentrate pro-
duced in most mines, though, is far below the standard acceptable in
the United States. Manganese is a war mineral whose production may
be increased, however, if the standards are greatly lowered.
Japanese manganese production figures (in metric tons): 1930,
19,588; 1935,10,391; 1940, 162,947; 1941,195,546; 1942,254,254;
1943, 342,884; 1944, 400,679. Imports: 1925, 53,537; 1930, 104,033;
1935, 217,089; 1939, 223,061. Import estimates for the fol1owing
years: 1940, 125,000; 1941, 80,000; 1942, 20,000; 1943, 20,000;
1944, none.
NICKEL
Most of Japan's nickel metal w?s importe through 194l. With
the exception of three years, the period 1932-1941 saw imports rang-
ing between 1,240 and 3,924 metric tons a year. The exceptions were:
1937, 6,511; 1938, 8,171; 1939, 12,208. Refinery production from
importeu nickel ore rose from 143 in 1939 to 617 in 1940; 1,823 in
1941; 1,592 in 1942; 1,748 in 1943, n 634 in 1944.
TUNGSTEN
Japan was far from producing enough tungsten ore at home for her
wartime needs, but as long as she commanded the coast of East Asia,
she could import the remainder. In 1943 her position was strong e-
nough so that she could ship about 2,5oo tons of concentrates to Ger-
many, presumably by submarine. If home production can be maintained
at the wartime rate, it should meet peacetime needs; but probably
much of the wartime production was heavily subsidized.
Japenese production of tungsten concentrate was 52 metric tons
in 1931; 89 in 1935; 677 in 1940; 957 in 1941; 994 in 1942; 856 in

Molybdenum
Japan's molybdenum supply is far less than her demend and it is
probable that much of the molybdenite has been produced at a very high
cost. .Japan will remain an importing country. A substantial part
of her requiremnts can probably be supplied by southern Korea.
Japan's molybdenite production in 1944 was 398 metric tons
compared with 176 in 1943; 89 in 1942; 49 in 1941, and 12 in 1940.
No data on Koren production since 199 was available but Korean
production that year was 115 metric tons. Imports: 1939, 4,246 met-
ric tons; 1940, 5,078; 1941, 273; 1942, 700; 1943, 200; 1944, 900.

29 November 45
Special Report
OJI PAPER MANUFACTURING COMPANY, LTD.
(Note: OJI, a MITSUI subsidiary, produced approximately
85 per cent of Japan's paper needs during the past 12 years.
OJI was also the sole manufacturer of newsprint. By this
virtual monopoly it is believed that, directly or indirectly,
OJI was able to persuade recalcitrant publications to follow
the government line.)
History:
OJI was established in l872 and is one of the oldest
joint-stock companies in Japan. In 1933 two of the largest
paper companies in Japan merged with OJI: Fuji Company,
with a subscribed capital of Yen 77,700,000 and 15 mills;
Karafuto Company, with Yen 70,O00,O00 and six mills. At
that time OJI owned 13 paper and pulp plants and had a
capital of Yen 65,916,650.
Resources:
In 1941 OJI had 33 paper and pulp mills and 11 subsi-
diaries. Besides manufacturing paper and pulp products,
OJI owned or controlled two investment companies, five
paper distributing firms, and was engaged in lumbering,
mining, railway transportation, hydro-electricity, machine
and tool manufacturing, and chemicals.
One of the principal sources of pulp was in Karafuto
(southern Saghalien), where eight mills and one pulp
company subsidiary are located. One mill and one paper
and ehcmical company subsidiary are located in northern
Korea, and three paper and one pulp manufecturing companies
are in southern Manchuria.
The remaining mills and subsidiaries are concentrated
in the home islands, with 18 mills, four subsidiaries in
Honshu; three mills, one subsidiary in Kyushu, and three
mills in Hokkaido.
Production:
Japan's annual paper production has been about one
million tons. In the first half of 194l OJI produced
500,000 tons, of which 400,000 was in paper products, and
100,000 in pulp.
In the

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
In the six-month period ending March 1945 OJI
produced less than 200,000 tons of paper and pulp. The
drop was attributed to the fire bombing which, OJI re-
ported, destroyed one-third of its productive machinery.
Newsprint:
Every newspaper and publishing firm in Japan has
been dependent upon OJI for its newsprint; however, to
help minimize the importance of its role, a complex
system of control was devised.
When rationing of newsprint was begun early in 1942,
at least five agencies were concerned with its allocation:
(1) Department of Commerce and Industry, which originated
directives, (2) Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, which
provided statistics on pulp resouroes and suggested con-
trols for its conservation, (3) Board of Information (Home
Ministry), which maintained close contact with publishers
through censorship control, and suggested the formation of,
(4) Newspaper Guild, composed of 55 representative news-
papers in Japan, which prorated newsprint, and (5) the
whole sale paper companies, from whom newsprint was drawn.
Several other agencies which exercised some juris-
diction over newsprint are excluded from this report in
order to avoid inextricable confusion.
GINJIRO Fujihara, former president of OJI, was Minister
of Commerce and Industry in the YONAI Cabinet (1940), and
other high OJI officials have held strategic government
pos ts from time to time.
Five of the eight paper distributing companies in the
Tokyo-Osaka areas, in which are located the largest news-
papers and publishing firms in Japan, are subsidiaries of
OJI. The remaining wholesale distributors are supplies by
OJI.
Present Supply:
With the abolishment of the censorship section of the
Board of Information, the biggest watch dog of the press
during the war, it is possible that less interference and
influence is being exerted on the Japanese press by the
government.
OJI, however, is still the source of supply, the
Department of Commerce and Industry sets the regulations,
the Newspaper Guild (League) prorates, and the wholesale
paper companies distribute the newsprint.
The following table shows the pre-war and post-war
allotment of newsprint to four leading Tokyo newspapers:

The Nippon Times, the only English language news-
paper in Tokyo, had an average pre-war circulation of
15,000. It dropped to 6,000 during the war. Its present
daily circulation is 48,000, with almost half going to the
occupation forces.
Note: The Japanese unit of newsprint measurement is
in reams. There are 25 reams in a roll; 500 sheets in one
ream, and four pages (eight sides) in one sheet.
Finances:
OJI began its business more than 70 years ago with a
capital of Yen 150,000. Following the merger in 1933 it
had Yen 149,988,000, and in 1941 it had been increased to
Yen 300,000,000. As of March 1945 it had a subscribed
capital of Yen 310,000,000.
According to a report submitted at the semi-annual
meeting of stockholders in March 1945, OJI had:
Assets
Y 725,920,000
Debits
219,750,000
Balance
506,170,000
Management:
Three men who have been leading figures in OJI since
1939 include GINJIRO Fujihara, a former president, KIKUJIRO
Takashima, a former president and currently adviser, and
TADASHI Adachi, formerly a managing director, who was
elected president of OJI in 1944.

Dear Mr. Thorp:
894.60/1-2346
This letter is directed to your attention
as liaison officer with the Publication Board. The
exploitation of scientific and industrial infor-
mation obtained from captured sources, directed by
the President in Executive Order 9604, contemplates,
I am sure, some technical investigations of Japanese
developments.
The War Department advises us that they
are agreeable to appropriate procedures but that
State Department concurrence must be obtained. To
date I have been unable to obtain any action within
the State Department and would appreciate your as-
aw
sistance.
I do not contemplate any wide-spread
exploitation such as we are carrying on in Germany.
However, I feel that a number of scientific and
CS/VJ
industrial specialists should be dispatched to
Japan as "ferrets" to make surveys, within their
fields, of Japanese advances and to determine what
further exploitation would be valuable. Any help
you can offer in getting State Department approval
to the sending of such missions will be appreciated.
Yours very truly,

Dear Ir. Thorp:
I have been in conversation with Dr. Fahs
894.60/2-146
of the State Department on the scientific and in-
dustrial exploitation of Japan. As you know, we
have embarked on a large scale collection of German
science and technology for the benefit of our
economy. I feel very strongly that an investigation
of the Japanese developments is also required if we
are to place the Presiaent's Executive Orders, 9568
and 9604, into full and beneficial effect.
It seems to me that appropriate scientists
and top flight technicians, limited in number, should
be dispatched to Japan as one-man "ferrets" to examine
and report upon valuable Japanese developments within
m
their specialized fields. This would be comparable
to the Technical Industrial Intelligence Committee
missions which are being carried out in Germany but
would be on a much more modest scale. As I presently
visualize this operation, approximately twenty men
would be dispatched and their resultant reports would
be disseminated through the Office of the Publication
CS
Board.
S/VJ
I am sure that Secretary Patterson, speaking
for the ar Department, would do all possible to imple-
ment this program if we can obtain State Department
acquiescence. Time is fleeting and science and industry
are becoming impatient. I would appreciate your efforts
894.60
in arranging some definite decision and recommendation
on this matter.
Elx
Yours very truly,
JC Sreen

MAY
February 20, 1946.
My dear Mr. Clayton,
As I explained to you yesterday, the Government of
India desire to send a small technical mission to Japan,
primarily for the purpose of examining developments in the
technique of certain selected industries in which Japan
had made conspicuous progress. By way of illustration, I
would mention the manufacture of ammonia and nitrogenous
fertilisers, silk, rayon and cotton textiles, steel rolling,
and the organisation of cottage industries.
For the balancing of our present one-sided
economy which, as you are aware, is predominantly agricultural,
it is of paramount importance that we should benefit, in our
programme of industrialization, from the experience of countries
0
which are industrially more advanced. Our technicians have
94.60/2-2046
already been sent for such study to the United Kingdom and
this country and also to Germany.
In response to a similar suggestion that I had
made to your Deputy, Mr. Willard Thorpe, on January 8th
last, I was asked whether the visit of these technical ex-
perts to Japan could wait until the countries interested in
obtaining reparations from Japan were invited to send groups
to Japan for the inspection and selection of machinery that
would suit the purposes of their respective Governments.
As would be evident, however, from what I have said in the
preceding paragraphs,the object of our present request has
no direct connection with reparations.
I am sure the Government of India will be grate-
ful if the Government of the United States and the Supreme
(0)
Commander would accede to their request. I need hardly Say
FILED
co-operate in every way with the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers.
Yours very sincerely,
894.

My dear Sir Girja:

desire of your government to send to Japan a small teohnical
mission. The matter was immediately brought to the attention
of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Pewers in Tokye frem
whom a reply has recently been received. The Supreme Commander
states that he would prefer pestponing the arrival of this
mission until (a) the solution of economic problems ralating
to Japan had progressed to the point where the introduction
854.60/2-2046
of a technical ission would not be premature, and (b) the
present acute shortage of accommodations in Japan had been
ameliorated.
As you know, the Far Eastern Comaission is currently
censidering the question of Haison between the Suprems
Commander for the Allied Powers in Tokyo and the Far Eastern
Commission in Washington. Included in this consideration
is the matter of sending specialized missions to Japan. When
the Far Eastern Commission has formulated the general prin-
ciples and procedures which are to govern these activities,
it should be appropriate for the Government of India to again
raise the question of dispatching to Japan a technical group
of the character you have envisaged.

FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
2516 MASSACHUSETTS AVENUE, N. W.
WASHINGTON 8, D. C.
RB/evc
27 May 1946
9ag/7
MEMORANDUM FOR
Mr. Gross
Mr. Lockwood
Mr. Whitman
Subject: Report on the Industrial Disarmament of Japan
by the National Engineers Committee
1.
I met with Mr. Proctor, Mr. Pirnie, Mr. Kirkpatrick,
on Friday morning to read and discuss a report on the Industrial
Disarmament of Japen, prepared by the National Engineers Committee
of the Engineers Joint Council. A luncheon at the Harvard Club
was held after this discussion, attended by Messrs. Jaeger, Reed,
Clark, Gates, as well as Proctor, Pirnie and Kirkpatrick.
2.
Colonel Frank and I read the report hurriedly,
anticipating that there might be some discussion concerning its con-
clusions. No discussion of the content of the report took place.
The purpose of our being there appeared to be for the committee to
obtain our views on when and how the report might best be cleared,
presented and used.
16
3.
It was decided that the report should be submitted
to the Secretaries of State, War and Navy by representatives of the
National Engineers Committee. Colonel Frank offered to make avail-
able the Public Relations resources of the War Department and to
arrange for whatever State Department publicity assistance as could
be offered. Since returning to Washington, Colonel Frank has spoken
5-2746
to Mr. Whiteman of the SNCC Secretariat regarding this matter and
in due course Mr. Whiteman will call upon Mr. Proctor and Mr. Pirnie
to work out details for release of the Report.
4.

that the Government would undertake responsibility for printing
80,O00 copies of the Report. Colonel Frank explained that funds
were not available for such a large printing but that the Army would
be glad to print sufficient copies to make possible distribution to
interested Government agencies, to SCAP, to the Far Eastern Commission
and to those members of the Engineers Joint Council primarily con-
cerned.

Colonel Frank is asking the War Department Public Relations officers

to handle this for him. JK should handle this operation for the State
Department. The National Engineers Committee is very anxious that its
Report obtain nationwide attention. It would, I believe, profer to

Conclusions, it might be transferred to the Appendix, leaving the
Conclusions free. If only minor statistical data in the Appendices

7.
Colonel Frank and I agree ith the Engineers that the
primary value of the Report springs from the fact that it was prepared
by an entirely independent group of American specialists who received
no official guidance in its preparation. Therefore, when the State
and Wer Departments study the Report, they should confine their
attention to security aspects of the paper and should not comment
upon discrepancies which mey appear between the Engineers Report
ano present official thinking on the matter of Japanese Industrial
Disarmament.
8.
The Report itself is an extraordinarily interesting
cocument and in general adopts tougher criteria for economic disarmament
than those being used by SiNCC at the present time. In recommending
removal or destruction of nitrogen fixation, synthetic oil, synthetic
rubber capacity, the Engineers indicate reliance upon selective in-

REPORT ON THE INDUSTRIAL DISARMAMENT OF JAPAN
Submitted to
THE SECRETARIES OF STATE, WAR & NAVY
by
THE NATIONAL ENGINEERS COMMITTEE
The National Engineers Committee was appointed by Engineers Joint
Council on February 12, 1945, to carry out the studies requested by the
thon Secrotary of Stato, Edward R. Stottinius, Jr., in his "memorandum
of requost" attachod to his lettor to Engineors Joint Council of Decem-
ber 28, 1944. That letter requosted tho proparation of "studies and
proposals bearing upon the industrial disarmamont of aggressor states."
The National Engineors Committee's roport on Industrial Disarmament
of Germany was submitted to tho Secrotaries of State, War and Navy.
through Engineers Joint Council, on September 24, 1945.
Engineers Joint Council, of which Mr. Alex D. Bailey 1s Chairman,
includes the President, Junior past President and Secretary of : American
Society of Civil Engineers, American Institute of Mining & Metallurgical
Engineers, American Society of Mechanical Engineers, Amorican Instituto of
Electrical Engineors, and Amorican Institute of Chemical Enginoers. Mr.
Malcolm Pirnio is Chairman and Mr. Robort M. Gates , Vice-Chaiman of tho
Executive Conmitteo.
Under date of Novenber 8, 1945, Assistant Socrotary of Stato, W. L.
Clayton wrote to Enginoors Joint Council oxpressing tho State Dopartment s
appreciation for tho report on Germany and roquesting "an examination of
the question of industrial disarmanent of Japan", as a rosult of which
the National Engineors Cormitteo was roconstituted for that purpose.
To fulfill the assignod objoctives. it has boon accopted by this
Committee as its function, to rocommond measures for the control of the
industrial lovel of Japan, suoh that her war potential and the threat

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
to future world peace and security by another Japanese aggression, will
be as largely as possible elfminated. To this end it has been consideréd
necessary to identify the critical war potential elements of Japanese
industry to be eliminated or controlled, so as to permit the non-war
potontial section of Japanese industry to function safely on a free
onterpriso basis.
This report has been proparod to meet the above stated objectivos
and the National Ingineers Committee submits it, encouraged by the fact
that the recommendations of its report on Germany were substantially
all included in the directive of the Allied Control Council of March 29.
1946, for the limitation and control of German industry.
FUNDAMENTAL CONSIDERATIONS FOR
INDUSTRIAL DISARMAMENT
Tho purpose of this report is to prescribe a plan for the control
of the Japanese nation which will eliminate its throat or challenge to
world poace for gonerations to come. In the formulation of any such
plan, positivo and continuing military and industrial disarmament must
be the ultimato goal but ruthless and indiscriminate dostruction of
all industry and of a sustaining national economy will defoat tho ro
establishment of peace and the eventual admission of Japan into the
counoils of peacoful nations. The plan proposod must be fim but also
just in the oye of the liberty loving people of the world. The complote
elimination of Japanese industry, leaving the nation to ako out a living
on agriculture and fishing. if possible, would croate social chaos and
suffering of such magnitude that onforcoment would be impossiblo, and
world opinion would repudiate the action.

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.97)
3
7
The seizure, destruction or elimination of all arms, ammunitionss
implements of war and combat ressele, and of facilities for their pro-
duction, was directed immediately upon the occupation of Japan and was
executed with order and dispatch. The surveys and studies to assemble
the data neceasary for an orderly control of civilian economy were begun
immediatoly and the Committee has had the benefit of the date thus acoumu-
lated. The work of the Committee began, therefore, with a study to
devolop a plan which would limit the industrial economy of Japan. seoure
industrial disarmament, and control the war potentials until long after
the occupation by the army has terminated.
The fundamental factors which have been considered are the psychol-
ogy of the Japanese people, the extent end limitation of the natural
resources particularly significant for wer. the control of industrial
facilities for processing and manufacture, the effect of controls upon
national oconomy and standards of living, and the will of the controll
ing nations to perpetuato their supervision.
Japanose Psychology Must Be Adjustod
Tho Japaneso attack upon Pearl Harbor and tho war of conquost in
tho Pacific was the fruition not only of yoars of industrial prepare-
tion, but gonerations of paychologioal and religlous indoctrination and
perversion. It would be folly to assume that the aggressive, brutal
instincts cultivated for years have been completely purgod by conquost
and by tho judgmonts of the courts of justice. This pervorsion and
fanaticiem must be corrected by means other than industrial disarmament,
but the plans for disarmament must be eo firm and enduring as to aheck
offectively any resurgence of militariem and of anbition for conquest.

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.98)
Any plan which is to be offootive must be sinplo and direot in oporation,
not unbearably opprossive to the connon people, and not repugnant to fair
inded and liborty loving people of the world.
4 Poaceful Econony in the Western Pacific Must Be Doveloped
For gonerations Japan has planned to nake the whole of Asia tribu-
tary to her econony and a neans of support to hor military aspirations
and dominance. The econonic dependence of Asia upon Japan is, therefore,
much greater than that of Central Europe upon Germany. In the pureuit
of these anbitions, Japan has succeoded in suppressing the industrial
strength of her neighbors as she has built up her own. The destruction
of her aggressive powor and the control of her potentials for future
war will be insufficiont, therofore, without tho sinultaneous develor
nent of industry, agriculturo and trade in and anong the neighbors of the
Japanose Islands.
The question of setting and onforcing regulations pertaining to
Japan propor, which will harnonize with policies adopted toward countries
liberated fron Japaneso donination, should receive careful consideration.
With rospect to both export and inport, the econonies of various nations
or districts have boon intor-related, and each new situation will pre
sent problens involving narket outlets, onploynent, neans of obtaining
nocossary supplies, and preservation of invosted capital in countries
outsido of Japan. If Japanese narkots are wholly end prenaturely closed
before others are developed, violonce nay be done to the industrial
econonies of nations fornerly depondont upon such outlots. On tho other
hand, during tho poriod of readjustnent and world-ahortages in cortain
itens, and until donestio industries are devoloped, it nay be found ad-
vantagoous to havo Japan supply at least a part of the requirenents of

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
populations in nearby sections of the world. These considerations will
affect final decisions relating to the production of commodities and
capital goods in Japan and will have a bearing upon the allowable ex-
tent of imports.
The end results, however, should contemplate a balanced economy for
Japan, devoid of the threat of war, and an expanding economy for her
neighbors in the Western Pacific.
Policies and Procedures for Control Must Be Simple
If the controls imposed over the industry of a conquered nation are
to be successful, they must be few in number, direct in application, and
require a minimum of policing. Policies and procedures endorsed by the
government of a victorious nation in the immodiate aftermath of the
horrors of war become soft and relaxing with waning memory. The re-align-
ments of power policies, the commercial pressures for the development of
trade, the sentimental expressions of humanitarians, all tend to weaken
and relax the limitations and controls for the suppression of national
expansion and conquest.
The controls herein recommended have been applied, therefore, to
the primary war materials and resources so as to be limited in number
and direct in application. They will require a minimum of policing,
which may be expected to remain long after large scale military occupa-
tion has been discontinued.
Primary Materials and Operations Should Bo Controlled
In the long list of components of modern war, thero are certain pri-
mery materials that have such particular properties and are required in
such quantitics, that war without them would be impossible. Without stoel

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.97)
-6
and steel alloys there would be no great fleets of ships and tanks and
trucks, nor grent assenblies of arnanent. Without aluninun there would
be no groat fleet of fighter, bonbing and torpedo planes. Without
fuels and lubricants, nodern air, land and sea power would be innobilised.
Without oxplosives to be conveyed to the targets, the instrunents of war
would be useless as weapons of destruction. These prinary considorations
innodiately suggest the basic plan for the destruction of war potential.
When a nation is ongaged in building a navy, or an air force, or
oquipping an arny, hundreds of naterials may becone critical itens. A
shortage of tungston or nolybdenun, or sone plesticizer for synthetic
rubber nay represent a bottlenock in needed production. Controls nay
becone necessery over the entire econony. When, on the other hand, it
is desired to perpetuate the disarnanent of a nation, this can be acoon-
plished with the control of a few prinary naterials and oporations.
Spocific Controle for Industrial War Potential
All industrial processes, regardless of whether they are orerating
for peacoful or aggressive purposes, are based upon three essential
factors without any one of which, production would be inpossible, to wits
raw naterials, processing equipnent and energy. The reconnendations for
the control of Japan's industrial war potential are, therefore, based on
these essential factors as they appear in the following industrioss
I. Mineral and netal industries
II. Hoavy chenical industries
III. Fuel industry
IV. Dlectrical power industry

COAT
Japan is adequately. though not abundantly, supplied with coal. Its
total coal reserves were estimated at 8 billion tons in l9l3 and re-esti-
nated at 16 billion in 1932. It is mostly bituninous coal of Miocene
Tertiary Age, though much analler enounts of both anthracite and lignite

1935 produotion) are in the island of Hokkaido, with Kyushu as the next
nost inportant in reserves. Honshu, the largest of the islands of Japan,
has a coal field in Hitachi and Awaki prefectures. but the quality is in-
ferior and the quantity uncertain.
In the decede 1912-192l the coal production in Japan proper increased
fron about 20 to 30 nillion tons. But a nost significant feature in the
total increase of coal resources was the extension of Japanese political

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
control over coal-producing areas outside its islands. Boginning with
Formosa, acquired from China in 1895, and followed by Karafuto (the
southern half of Sakalien) in 1905, Korea, annexed by Japan in 1910, and
Manchuria, over-run in l93l and set up as a puppet state, the coal avail-
able to Japan was greatly increased.
The basic question, however, is whether the coal-producing capacity
left in Japan will be adequato to meet the needs of the post-war sconomy.
Apparently it will, but the answer to the question cannot be certainly
deduced from the production capacity data. The post-war coal neods will
be determined by the volume and activity permitted to the industries
which are the principal consunors of coal, and by the railway traffic and
shipping resulting fron such activities. Sinilarly. the needs for coking
coal will be fixed by tho volume of netallurgical activity pernitted, and
the roquirenents for bunker coal by the shipping activity allowed.
If, whon theso quantities are doternined, there renains surplus

advisability of renoving the surplus production equiprent and delivering
it to China as a part of roparations, or to retain such surplus equipment
to support tho oxport of coal.
Whatevor course is followod, measures should be instituted to onsure
that Japaneso coal is not dunped into Chinese and other narkets as a
neans of increasing trade balances at a loss. Current estinates suggest
that recent Japanhose output has beon supported by subsidies up to 40%
of the cost of production. China will need all the export narkets for
coal available as a help to trade balances in its post-war recovery and
developnent. The Japanese coal industry should not be allowed to interfere
with this. Bocauso different industries need different grades of coal

tion of a most important munition of war which
puts them in the same class as plants for the
production of explosives.
(b)
The Japanese synthetic oil industry has been
maintained through subsidy as an implement of
war. This industry should be eliminated. not
only as a source of supply of material essen-
tial to mechanized and air warfare, but because
the oquipnent of this industry can, with certain
nodifications, be converted to the production of
fixed nitrogen.

(c) Processos for the production of synthetic fuols
requiro much hoavy equipnent and a large invest-

snall labor force
The cost of synthetic oils fron coal is considerably nore than that
of inportod potroleun products for Japan under a peacetine econony.
The maintenance of this industry would be a burden to tho Japanese
people and its olinination will improve the Japanese living standard.
Japanese equipnent for synthetic fuel plants is of doubtful repare
tions value as nost of it is of obsolete design.
Low tenporature carbonization plants nay be pernitted because with-
out hydrogenation or Fischor-Tropsch plants, only snall anounts of
notor fuel or fuel oil can be realized as by-products of such plants.
Their prinary product would be low-tenperature coke which is ueseful for
donostic heating.

SOLENTIFIC RESEARCH
All of the control neasures thus far outlined have been directed
toward the regulation of onergy, oxisting materials and equipnont and

should bo roduccd to as mall units as can offoctivoly porform tho work

NUCLEAR ENERGY
Intornational control of raw natorial sourcos and industrial facil-
itios for tho namufacturo of atonic bosbs and rolatod woapons is boing
studiod currently by tho Socurity Council of the Unitod Nations, and
prosunably its procoduros will offoctivoly disarm Japan as woll as all
othor aggressor nations. If the plan roconmondod by our Stato Dopartnont
is accoptod univorsally, ownorship of all ninos and othor sourcos of

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.99)
fissionable materials is automatically taken by the international control
agency which will likewise own and'operate all primary facilities for the
release of atomic anergy.
The complete elimination and prohibition of all activities relating
to research and development on the manufacture of atomic weapons, and in
connection with all phases of nuclear energy, must be rigidly enforced
as the first essential of any program for the disarmament of Japanese war
potential industries.
REGULATION AND CONTROL OF FUTURE CONSTRUCTION
Evasion of the regulations and controls horein recommended, intended
for illegal production of war potential. raw materials, would require large
installations, as for example, facilities for the production of fixed
nitrogon, synthotic oil, aluninun, stcel and atonic energy.
Energy controls night be offective for the regulation of established
industry, but new plants will be built, and it is, therofore, necessary
to assure that such plante nay not be convertible into war plants. Thore-
fore, the rigid control of all new construction should be naintained
through the issuance of pernits by a Board of Construction Control, con-
posed of experts in all potentials of war production.
It is recognised that the principles of atonic energy nay be known
to Japanese scientists during the period of contenplated tonure of these
regulations, but it is believod that control of such developnent will be
readily obtaincd through the rigid regulation of all construction and
control of power roquirenents, to preclude the possibility of construotion
of plants for the developrents of such atonic energy.

=== Page 92 === (confidence 1.00)
SUPPLEMENTAL CONTROLS WITH SIMPLIFIED IMPORT LICENSING
To be completely effective the control of raw materials critical
to war potential industries must include an import licensing system
to regulate the nitrogen imported as fertilizer, petroleum and liquid
fuels, aluminum, steel, and any other materials which might contribute
to war potential. This system should be operated on the basis of a
"Controlled Materials Plan" requiring every individual, concern or
manufacturer handling any of these items to state in complete detail the
quantities and type of material required, the nature and disposition of
the finished product, and the scrap resulting from fabricating operations.
Any of the materials licensed by this system would be considered "in
bond" and the possession of any such materials unauthorized by the proper
documents would be illegal. Such a system is considered essential to
eliminate attempts to stock-pile these materials, especially scrap, and
to counteract the inevitable effort of Japan to obtain participation in,
or control of, foreign plants for the purpose of shipping critical
materials into Japan under the guise of Japanese ownership.
PROCESSING AND FABRICATING REGULATIONS
Supplemental control of certain specific equipment and the scale of
operation, within the restricted portion of war potential industries re-
maining in Japan, are necessary to eliminate from Japan some tools and
machinery that could be readily converted to the production of war imple-
ments, or that could fabricate materials of such shape or size that they
could be readily converted to war implements.
Such regulations apply to the tools and machinery of the aluminum

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.98)
-36
fabricating and steel industries or any other industries in which such
steps are considered necessary. Specific details should be set up by
experienced engineers of the Control Council with firsthand kmowledge of
the type of equipnent in Japanese plants and the oxtent to which the
plants will be pernitted to function.
CONCLUSION
If the Control Council in Japan will institute and naintain effeo-
tive controls of power production and distribution, and industrial plant
construction to
(1)
Prohibit the production of synthetic liquid fuele:
(2)
Prohibit the production of aluninun and nagneeiun;
(3)
Prohibit the use of nuclear onergy:
(4)
Limit and control the synthetic fixation of nitrogen;
(5)
Linit and control the production of sulphuric acid and calciun
carbidog
(6)
Linit and control the capacities and production of steel and
steelproducts plants;
(7)
Linit the capacities and production of copper.
such procedures will establish a considerable measure of assurance against
the danger of Japanese rearneanent.
Eaployment of the total Japanese peacetine labor force will be re-
duced about five porcont by the prohibitions and controls herein recon
mended. Thore should bo no serious problen in the re-onployment of such
a emnall percontago of labor in Japan's peacetine agriculture and consumer
goods industries.
These reconmended procedures are not intended as a substitute for
the protection afforded by an adequate police force of the United Nations

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.92)
in Japan. Freedom from fear of rearmament will be attained only so long
as such a police force remains in authority.
For the guidance and direction of the'policing forces, there should
be established a group of technological specialists who will keep in-
formed as to Japanese industrial and researoh activities.

COPPER
Next to steel, coprer was one of the nost critical netals in the
Japanese ninerals econony. The Japanese developed a complete technology
for mining and smelting copper long before the restoration, but soon
after introduced foreign practice in metallurgical operations and before
World War I, built up a substantial self-contained copper industry.
In the years between the two World Wars, imports were small, except in
1926 to 1928 whon they averaged 12,500 tons.
Consumption stood at about 70, 000 tons from the middle 20's to
1933 when it began to increase rapidly for use in the armament program.
By 1937 the total consumption of copper had risen to 150,000 tons, and
it kept at approximately that figure until the increasing difficulties
in shipping incident to the war cut down imports.
Of the domestic production, approximately 60,000 tons were from
ores mined in Japan proper. The romainder was fron ores mined in
Formosa, Korea and clsewhere. These were supplemented by imports of
blister copper largely fron the United States. In the 30's, Japan built

the outbreak of the war with Ohina was also building up an export busi-

TIN
Tin deposits are found at various points in Japan, especially on the
Island of Kyushu, where tin mining is an ancient industry in the south.
In 1909 the Mitsubishi Companies found tin in the Aktonobe Mino on Honshu,
and in later years they and others developed other deposits on both islands.
None of the doposits are large, and in 1931 Japanese production was 1,015
tons, with imports of 3,257 tons. In 1940 the corresponding figures wore
1. 620 and 9,840 tons. All this was consumed at home, save for minor amounts
exported as tinfoil, and in various manufactures.

It will be necossary for Japan to continue to import either nickol,
metal or ores, or both. It would scen reasonable to impose no obstacles
to either, but to permit the free play of conpetition to determine the
results.
This will afford the most ready field for application of the principle
of a peacetime economy, based on importation of a minimum of raw materials
and applying to them the available energy fron hydro power, and the avail-
able lebor, skill and managerial ability.
In reorienting the industry of Japan toward peace instead of war,
certain principles should be kept in mind. In view of the fact that
-15-

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.98)
A P P E N D I XII (Cont'd.)
Japan will need to import food, there should be no unnocossary importa-
tion of othor raw matorials. In othor words, where there is possibility
of manufacturing on tho basis of donestic raw naterials, that is to bo
proforrod. Also, it is preferable that Japan import raw materials
rather than finished or semi-finished goods, so that, to the maximum,
the power, fuel, labor, skill and managoment of the country bo used in
the production of finishod goods for domostic consumption or oxport.
Finally, rostrictions should be applied on inport of raw naterials
nocossary to hoavy industrios rather than to tho nanufacturo of consumer
goods, if tho objoct of offccting tho disarnanont of Japanoso industry
nocossary to prevent war is to bo net.
MANGANESE
The most necessary of the minerals in the ferro-alloy group is
manganese, although its use in alloys is minor. It is employed mainly
as a scavenger in making steel, and for this purpose it is essontial to
uso 12 to 14 pounds in the making of each ton of steel. Most of this is
carried off in the slag, but the importanco of tho small amount which ro-
mains in the stocl has como to a now approciation. Manganoso also has
inportant applications as a non-notallic in chonical industrics. Whilo
thore are munerous doposits in Japan, thoy are snall and tho donostic
supply is wholly inadequate. Ore has long been inportod from othor East
Asiatic countrios, particularly India, Malaya and tho Phillipinos. In
tho prowar yoars, 193l to 194l, inclusivo, domostic production incronsed
from 12, 849 metric tons to 92,000 tons, while tho import roso fron 40,316
to approxinatoly 175,000 tons. Exports aro not sigrificant. Fcrro-
manganese production 1s reported to have grown fron 34, 710 tons in 1935
to 68, 553 in 1941. With tho stoel industry cut back to ono third of tho
-16

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
A P P E N D I XII (Cont'd.)
maximum wartime output, it is doubtful that Japan can be self-sustaining
in manganese ores for any long period, although it could for a few years-
If it be found desirable, an interdiction on manganese imports for 
limited term of years may be exercised.
CHROMITE
The Japanese are better supplied with this minoral than with manga-
nese ores. In 1931 domestic production was 9,675 metric tons, with no
recorded imports. In 1941 the production had grown to 50,212 tons, with
7,500 tons of imports. Thereaftor, imports apparently incroased rapid-
ly and large tonnages of high-grade ore were looted from the Philippines.
Chromium has many essential uses in the arts and industries and has been
rapidly rising in importance, particularly following development of
chromium plating and the manufacture of so-called stainless steels, which
by reason of their great strength and stiffness are competing strongly
with the light metals in many fields. No great damage would result to
the country by forbidding imports of chromite ore for a limited term of
years, although it is not important as a war potential material.
TUNGSTEN
This is another of the metals important in making alloy steels,
particularly those used for cutting tools. The amount needed is not
large and to a considerable extent molybdenum can be substituted for it.
Domestic production of tungsten ores in Japan is unimportant, and the
steel makers have dopended largoly on ores fron Korea with imports fron
China, Burma and other East Asian sources easily available. Japanese
production of tungstic oxide, 55 percont concentrate in 1931, is given
as 52 tons, and in 1941 as 957 tons. In the same years, Korea produced
-17 -

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
A P F E N D I X II (Cont d.)
16 and an estimated 2,600 tons, respectively. The Korean concentrates
are calculated at 60 percent WO3. Wartime production seems to have been
heavily subsidized, but ordinary output may be sufficient to meet domes-
tic needs of the reorganized steel industry, although this is by no means
certain and small imports in reasonable quantity might be safely per-
mitted.
MOLYBDENUM
Molybdenum has been produced in Japan, but almost entirely from im-
ported ores, especially from Korea. The amounts involved are not large,
and there is no present knowledge which warrants belief that Japan could
supply itself in the future. Recognition of the value of molybdenum in
alloy steels has been greatly enhanced by wartime exporiences, espocial-
ly in the United Statos.
Minor ferro-alloy minerals -- vanadiun, cobalt and others -- have
been used in Japan, as elsewhere, but the tonnages and values involved
are not important.
Thile the ferro-alloy minerals are essential to the continuanco of
industry in Japan, all the more so if steel consumption is to be chan-
nelled into the manufacture of consumer goods, the actual tonnages in-
volved are not large and any necessary control of their movement should
be easily effected. Electric furnaces of one type or another, themselves
large consumers of special steels, have been largely used in the reduc-
tion of these ores and the production of the ferro-alloys, so that a
system of licenses would control the largest part of the production.
However, blast furnaces may also be used so that such control would not
be complete and a better means would be by licensing imports. The
-18 -

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.99)
A P P E N D I X II (Cont'd.)
simplest way to make this effective would be by control of the customs
Maritime
in some such manner as the Chinese/xrxma Customs were long managed
under British supervision. As previously suggested, however, it is
doubtful whether any, and at most more than a temporary, control is
desirable in the field of ferro-alloys or the ores from which thoy are
made.
ANTIMONY
Smelting of antimony ores has been carricd on in Japan for nearly
three-quarters of a century, although it has been based mainly on the
importetion of ores because deposits in Japan are few, small and lean.
The ore used came largely from China, the world's chief producer, but
after the war with China began, Indo-China became an important source of
supply. The whole production was small, from 2,500 to 3,500 tons per
year, and can hardly have done more than meet domestic demands. Neither
Korea nor Manchuria has been a producer of importance. Certain antimony
compounds and alloys are used in modern industry and will continue to be
needed in Japan.
MINOR IETALS
Arsenic, bismuth and a few other metals, or their conpounds, are
regularly recoverod as by-products from copper, gold, silver and lead
snelting. Arsenic is used for making insecticides, and both it and bis-
muth have pharnaceutical uses. About half the arsenic produced has been
exported. Future production will depend on the activity in non-ferrous
smelting, and no good purpose would seem to be served by restricting it.
Mercury is produced in small quantities in Japan, but imports have
been the main source of supply. The consumption rose rapidly in the year
of war preparation. Any restriction found necessary can be accomplished.
-19-

=== Page 118 === (confidence 1.00)
A P P E N D I X II (Cont'd.)
by control of imports. Neither Korea, Formosa nor Lanchuria is an im-
portant producer, although a small amount of mercury comes from the first
named country.
PRECIOUS METALS
The Japanese Empire was an important producer of gold and silver,
although slightly less than half the output came from mines in Japan
itself. Formose yielded a minor amount and Korea slightly more than
half. The larger part of both the gold and the silver was obtained as
a by-product of non-ferrous smelting, the copper or lead in the ore being
used as a collector in the furnace work in the usual manner. Independent
production of neither gold nor silver was important, although there is
a limited amount of placer mining. In Manchuria plaçer mining is more
important than lode mining. The figures for gold production through the
