=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.97)
RPORM NO. 64
TO
: A-H - Mr. Gross
DATB:
September 19, 1946
FROM : JK - Mr. Whitman
suject: Assured Production Capacity Levels for Japan.
Please refer to my memorandum of September 18, to
General McCoy covering the present status of this paper
in Committee no. 2 with the FEC.
-t the time of the receipt of the message from
SCAP urging immediate FEC action, consideration was
iven to issuing an interim directive. At that time
I opposed such action or even the threat of such action
as extremely difficult to defend since once issued it
would have to be binding and the United States Govern-
ment would have to maintain the position taken, if
necessary by use of the veto.
In view of the slow progress of the paper in the
FEC now I think we might consider an interim directive
establishing levels at 75% of the figures in the United
States paper. A possible exception of sulphuric acid
which as relatively the most controversal item might
be put at 50%. I would like to be authorized to use
the threat of an interim directive on such lines at the
next meeting of the Economic Committee. I would do so
only if in my judgment at that time, I felt the
Committee was being unreasonable in stalling action
on the paper and if it appeared that such a threat
would not prejudice the general position of the United
States.

SECRET
13 August 1946
Memorandum by the State Member, SWNCC
REQUEST FOR DETERMINATION OF "U. S. POLICY ON INTERIM
REMOVALS AS REPARATIONS OF JAPANESE STEEL ROLLING CAPACITY"
Attached is a proposed U.S. policy position on interim removals
as reparations of Japanese steel rolling capacity.
While the present plan is to obviate the necessity for further
consideration of interim reparations by either the SWNCC or the FEC,
in order that the U.S. may devote the cntire energy of its limited
staff to consideration of final rcparations removals with which the
proposed Reparations Conference will bo expected to deal, it is
felt that an exception in the case of stecl rolling capacity is in
order.
The Chincse, who would be the principal beneficiaries of roll-
ing capacity removals, have been pushing for an carly dccision in
the FEC on interim removals of such capacity.
There is an obvious
hiatus in thc U.s. policy on iron and stecl in that it did not
specifically include rolling capacity. Moreover, interim removals
of iron and stecl capacity, unloss cccompanied by removals of roll-
ing capacity, would be of no benefit to countrics like China where
lack of surficient rolling capacity is already the bottlencck in
their iron and steel industry. In FEC 059/17, 26 June 1943, the
U.S. agrecd to consider rolling capacity in a soparate paper.
The rocomnendations in the attached proposal are not in con-
flict with the Comprehensive Report of Ambassador Pauley, or with
the views of SCAP.
In viow of these considerations and the desirability of an
early decision in order to preserve harmonious relations in the FEC,
and particularly with China, it is suggested that after obtaining
the opinion of the JCs from a military point of view, the recom-
mendation be conaidoned be the
SLNCa

=== Page 380 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
ENCLOSURE
INTERIM REMOVALS:
STEEL RCLLING CAPACITY
THE PROBLEM
1. To determine U.S. policy on interim reparations removals
of Japanese steel rolling capacity.
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. See Appendix "A".
DISCUSSION
3. See Appendix "B".
CONCLUSIONS
4. The following action should be taken as a part of the
program on removals as interim reparations:
Steel Rolling Industry
(Definition: Plants and establishments engaged in pro-
ducing basic steel shapes, such as rails, rods, bars, tubes,
plates, strips, sheets, and structural shapes, by rolling,
drawing, and extruding steel ingots.)
That portion of Japan's steel rolling capacity in excess
of that required to produce a balanced annual output of
3,000,000 metric tons of rolled steel products should be
made inmediately available as reparations.
RECOMMENDATIONS
5. It is recommended that:
a. The State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee, after
securing the comments of the Joint Chiefs of Staff from a
military point of view, approve the conclusions in paragraph
4.
b. This report be transmitted for information to the
Joint Chiefs of Staff, State, War and Navy Departments, and
the United States representative on the Far Eastern
Commission for his information.
c. No parts of this document be made available for
public release,

FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. Reduction of Japanese War Potontial, SWNcc 302, Paragraph
8, War-Supporting Industries:
"a. Over-all Policy:
As a supplcmentary measure designed further to reduce
Japan's industrial war potential, that portion of existing
capacity in selected war-supporting industries which is in
excess of that necessary to ncct the peaceful needs of the
Japanose people, as defined below, should be made available
for renoval as reparations.
"b. Critori. Underlying Over-All Policy:
A doternination should be nade by the Far Eastern Con-
nission as to the categorics and volune of industrial produc-
tive capacity in war-supp rting industries which are in excess
of the peaceful needs of the Japanese people. For the above
purposc, these peaceful needs should be defined substantially
as the standard of living prevailing in Japan during the
years 1930-34 (1.e., average Japanese per capita consumption
during these years).
Satisfaction of the peaceful ncods of
the Japanece people should therefore be understood to roquire
sufficient capacity in war-supporting industrics for:
(1) support of donestic consuner goods industries
functioning collectively nt a lovel ossential to nainten-
ance of the 1930-34 11ving standard;
(2) paynent, through exports, for inports neoded to
sustain the 1930-34 1iving standard;
(3) construction, repair, and naintenance of transport,
housing, public utility, industrial, and other facilitics
to the degree that they are essential to naintonanco of
the 1930-34 living standard.
"c. Application of Critoria:
(1) The following spccific industries are considered
as being 'war-supporting', for purposcs of the industrial
renovals proposed in this paper:

On 28 June 1946, views of the United Kingdon vis-a-vis the Chinese
proposal, were subnitted by the United Kingdon Menber of Connittee
No. 1, Far Eastern Connission:
"1. It has been proposed in the Reparations Connitteo that
part of Japan's steel rolling nill capacity should be nade
available for clain under the Interin Reparations Renovals
Progron.
The United Kingdon's view on this proposal, while
faverable in principle is that prelininary study is required
before exact proposals can be franed, and it is suggosted
that a sub-connittee should be appointed for the purpose.
"2. The reasons for the United Kingdon view are sot
out below:
PRIMARY ROLLING MILLS
Infornation is required on the size of Japan's bloon-
ing and slabbing nills and where they are installed. Up to
1939 1t was generally understood that Japan was unable to
produce these nills donestically and had purchased all such
equipnent fron abroad.
There is sone evidence to show that
the production of rolled steel products tended downward rather
than upward after 1939 and that therefore the Japanese nay
not have been able to fabricato these nills during the war
yoars.
The existence of a secondary rolling mill capacity of
about 8.5 nillion tons would suggest that there were suffi-
ciont prinary nills to deal with this output, but it is pos-
sible that in nany placcs the size of ingots cast was very
nuch roduced so as to cnable sec ndary roughing nills to take
care of the rolling, thereby by-passing the blooning nills.
It is, therefore, inportant that an investigation be made of
Japaneso steel rolling practice before it can be doternined
what prinary nill capacity should be retained for the future
pornitted operation of the industry.
Until this is done no
satisfactory estinate can be made of the available surplus of
this particular type of rolling plant.

SECONDARY ROLLING MILLS
Japan's secondary rolling nill capacity is believed to
anount to about 8,646, 000 tons per annum traceable to 51 aif-
ferent plants. Of this anount 1,206,000 tons are owned by
34 concerns which have an annual rolling capacity of under
100,000 tons. They derive their supplies of steel alnost
entirely fron their own electric steel furnaces. Sone of the
renainin 17 plants, with a capacity of over 100, 000 tons,
are not fully integrated and have to purchase ingots or bloons
fron other plants which produce nore steel than they can
proccss.

SECRET
APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
INTRODUCTION
1.
The proposnls contained in this paper are designed to es-
tablish the neasures to be applied in effecting a reduction of
Japancso stccl rolling capacity for reasons of security and as
interin reparations renovals.
2.
The industry dealt with in this paper is an essential part
of the iron and steel industry which in turn is a part of the -
gregate of industrial facilities built up by Japan for the purpose
of wagin: large scale war.
Ratio
of
Rollcd Steel Products to Iniot Steel:
3.
During the period 1930-1934, Japanese steel rolling exper-
ience indicated a ratio of approximately 84s of rolled products to
total steel ingot production. Use of steel ingot for castings and
forgings was relatively minor, accounting for slightly less than
100,ooo metric tons of steel ingot consumption during the same
period.
This ratio tended to decline with the increased use of
higher crade steel products.
On technological grounds, therefore,
it seems reasonable to estimate thc future peace time ratio at
about 8o% which would still mean fairly low-grade rolled steel.
Consistency of Proposal with Existing Proposals (Appendix "A")
4。
It is obvious that policy with respect to removals of
Japanese rolling equipment should be related to the policy on re-
moval of steel ingot capacity, since it is assumed that Japan will
not export ingot.
This relationship should be fairly precisc in
the case of final removals, in the sense that Japanese rolling
capacity to be finally retained should be adequate to process the
amount of steel ingots (less forgings and castings) that can be
produced by finally retained stecl capacity. For interim renoval
purposcs, however, a rough relationship is sufficient so long as
rolling capacity is not less than the cmount necessary to process
the amount of ingot that can be produced.

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.97)
S E C R E T
a. The interim steel ingot capacity of 3,250,000 metrio
tons annually, as recommended in SWncc 236/10 would, at the 80%
ratio, require the interim retention of rolling capacity cap-
able of producing 2,600,000 mctric tons annually of basic
stecl shapes (or 3. 25 million metric tons X 80%). The 1nterim
retention of rolling capacity capable of producing 2,775,000
metric ons annually of basic shapes is not sufficicntly
higher to be inconsistent.
b. The implied assured rolling capacity necessary to handle
the recommended assured ingot capacity of 2,o0o,000 metric
tons annually (SWNCC 236/19), would, at the 80% rate, produce
1,60o,000 metric tons of rollcd basic shapes annually. The
higher figure of 2,775,000 reconmended in this paper for
intorin purposes does not, ticrefore, conflict with the policy
implied in SWNCC 236/19.
c. On the same ground, the proposal is consistent with
SCAP'c recomnendation on assured capacity of 2,500,o00 metric
tons of steel ingot capacity (subject to a possible 20% re-
duction); or an implied assured capacity to produce 2,000,000
notric tons of rolled steel (also subject to a possiblc 20%
rcduction).
d. The Chinese proposal in thc Far Eastern Conmission
reconnonds the retention in Japen, for interin purposes, of
3,50o,000 netric tons of stecl ingot capacity and of steel
rolling capacity sufficient to process that amount of ingot.
At the 80% ratio, this would mean the interim retention of
rolling capacity sufficient to produce 2,80o,00o netric tons
of rolled steel, or 25,ooo metric tons more than the amount
proposed in this paper. It is bclieved the Chinese Govornnent
will readily agree to the slightly lower figure, which would
add a snall increase to interin reparations availabilities.
C. The U.K. view in the Far Eastern Commission is favorable
to the principle of relating rolling capacity to ingot
capacity, and in this connection tentatively suggests the
intorin retention of secondary rolling capacity sufficient to

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
5
produce 2,775,0oo metric tons of basic steel shapes annually.
With rospect to primary rolling capacity, which is interpretod
to nean rough rolling of steel ingot into bloons, billets, and
slabs, tho U.K. paper raises tho qucstion as to whether Japan
had sufficient prinary capacity to support her secondary roll-
ing mills, or in practicc, was forccd to make snaller ingots
which could be used in secondary rolling mills without having
to be first processed by prinary rolling. The U.K. suggests
that Japanesc practice in this respect be investigated before
dotermining the amount of prinary capacity that should be
retained.
There is no inconsisteney, however, between the recon-
nondation in this paper and thc U.K. suggestions. Tho proposal
in this paper agrces exactly with the U. K. tentative suggcs-
tion of "retention of a finishcd stool rolling nill capacity
of 2, 775, 0oo tons, " which tho U.K. menber of Connittee No. 1
has intorproted to nean production of rolled steel to that
anount; and by approaching the problen by way of rolling
capacity sufficient to producc 2,775,0oo netric tons annually
of basic shapes, avoids the difficulty posed by the U.K. paper
of doternining in the FEc how much primary capacity would be
involved.
For interin renoval purposes, it does not oppeer
neccssery that the FEc distinguish between prinary and second-
ary rolling in order to approve the interin removal as repara-
tions of all Japanese rolling capacity over and above that
roquircd (cither as prinary or as secondary capacity) to
produce 2,775,00o netric tons of basic shapos annually.
Other Considcrations
5.
Tontative conclusions arrived at fron analyses of the
Japanose iron and steel industry which the Office of Rescarch and
Intelligencc of the State Departnent is at present naking, indicate
a probablc future peacetine need in Japan for about 2,7oo,o0o netric
tons annually of rolled steel. Since this figure includes an
anount of rolled steel equivalent to 1930-34 annual per capita

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.97)
`S E C R E T
consunption of steel for shipbuilding, corrected for population
increase, and since it is doubtful if Japan will be pernitted
steel shipbuilding on this scale, the final estinate of total
Japanese peacetine needs for rolled steel nay well be less than
2,700,000 netric tons. The reconnendation in this paper for
interin retention of capacity to produce 2,775,000 metric tons
therefore leaves some margin for further and final renovals of
steel rolling capacity.

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.97)
Enclomure No 3 to despute 2318 2ct 301946
I ondon, L.
PAER:   S DN
LOWOY
DATE:
 29 1940
Japanese Competition
The Government is taking a sensible
attitude about the future of Japanese
industry, though it will disappoint
some British manufacturers. Japan is
a highly populated industrial country
dependent on imports for most of her
raw materials. To pay for them she
must export manufactured goods. If
that were prevented she would have
to have some form of subsidy from
abroad—which Britain at least is
certainly not prepared to give.
Japanese industry will be gravely
crippled in any case by the restric-
tions which must be placed on
industries of military importance. Sir
Stafford Cripps, in his statement
yesterday, insisted that it was not
practicable to get rid of Japanese
competition in our export markets by
a further "surgical óperation" on
non-military industries. Japan must
be free to restore her export trade in
such things as textiles. That would
probably be the policy even if the
British Government did not like it.
The decision rests with the Far
Eastern Commission meeting in
Washington, and the American Gov-
ernment would oppose any plan to
stifle Japanese trade. A year ago there
was talk of turning Japan into an agri-
cultural country, but since then the
American authorities there have been
trying to get the lighter industries
going. No source of supply can be
kept idle when the Far East's need
for goods, and especially for clothes,
is as great as it is at present. British
policy should aim at ensuring that
future Japanese trade is not helped,
as it was before the war, by low
wages and unfair trading methods.
It should be possible to impose
conditions which will prevent such
methods as subsidies and the manipu-
lation of exchange rates. But, as Sir
Stafford said, we cannot lay down any
particular standard of wages for
Japan. The remedy there must be
slower. The pressure of the trade
unions which are now being formed
should gradually raise the wages of
Japanese workers.

COMMITTE:
MILITARY AFFAIRS
Congress of the United States

December 2, 1946
DEC3,1946
894.60/12-246
Honorable John H. Hilldring,
Assistant Secretary of Stete (for Occupied Areas)
Stete Department,
Washington, D.C.
J.
My dear General Hilldring:
I have had an inquiry from a friend of mine in Buffalo, New York
calling to my attention an article which has appeared in the
Wall Street Journal of November 25th which states that the Military
Government authorities are revising the china industry in Germany
and Japan and that a subsidiary of the R. F.C. is importing ceramics
so manufactured without duty and is putting them on the American
market in direct competition with goods manufactured in this country.
In view of your present assignment with the State Department I
assume you are familiar with some of the details which are being
worked out both in Germany and in Japan in getting their industries
back into production.
At any rate would you be good enough to let me know just what the
situation is in this connection.

N. W.
DATE: December 19, 1946
FROM : JK - Mr. Whitman
sUBJECT: Revival of Japanese soda ash industry to meet U.S. requirements.
Information coming into the Department in recent weeks has indicated
that a shortage of soda ash in this country is severly restricting pro-
duction in many basic industries in the U.S. Soda ash is essential in the
production of chemicals, pulp, paper, aluminum, iron and steel textiles,
soap, water-sofeners, petroleum products and all kinds of glass. Libbey-
Owens-Ford Glass Company is urging action which can be taken to increase
the supply and it is believed the Kaiser interests are also interested in
finding new sources of soda ash.
At present there is in Japan a very large amount of soda ash capacity
46.8
which is not being used (Production is less than 3,Oo0 tons per month and
capacity over 800,000 tons annually). Part of this capacity is probably
inoperable because of damage. One plant has already been set aside for
reparations but other plants are not in production due to shortages of raw
materials and the general low level of Japanese industrial activity.
In the case of Gernany, a number of arrangements are under way for the
provision of raw materials by private concerns with payment to be made from
2-1946
the output of German industry. It is believed that similar schemes would
be feasible in Japan. Before discussing the matter further with American
corporations it is necessary to get SCAP's general reaction to the idea.
Accordingly the attached cable is suggested:
DRAFT CABLE
Inquiry from two important American firms indicate critical shortage
soda ash in US in 1947. Reactivation of Japanese soda ash capacity
believed to be one possibility of making up  US deficit. us oompapies

LIEBEY-GNENS-FGRD GLASS COPANY

December 13, 1946
The Memorable W. L. Clayton
Under Seeretary of State
Department of State
Washington, D. C.
Dear Secretary Clayton:
The critical shertage of soda ash (sodium carbonate) in the United
States has seriously restricted production in many basic industries
simee the war, and will continue to do so throughout 1947 unless
maximm rasoureafalness is employed by our government with the
ceeperatien of the industries most vitally affected. Soda ash, as
you kmow, is a basic material without which many impertant American
industries cannot even operate. It is absolutely essential in the
production of many chemicals, pulp and paper, alumimm, iren and steel,
textiles, soap, water softeners, petroleum produots and all kinds of
glass, inoluding automobile safety glass, containers, fibre glass and
building glass so necessary to the housing program.
There are several possible ways in which you and your Department with the
ceoperation of the Commerce and War Departments can assist in ineremsing
the supply of soda ash for the United States. It is for this reasen
that I am addreesing you and sending similar letters to Seeretary
Patterson and Secretary Harriman.
If agreeable to you, it would seem desirable for you to designate some
one in your Department to cooperate with the other interested depart-
ments and agencies so as to pull tegether the individuals required to
carry out the following program:
1) To the fullest extent possible and practicable, exports of soda ash
frem Gormany to the United States should be authorized to meet the
oritical needs of this country.
There is, for example, the Mathes and Weber soda ash plant at
Duisburg which has been decided to be available fer reparations.
It would take anywhere from a year to eighteen months to move sush
a plant, reinstall it, and get it back into produetion. Therafore,
it seems a wise pelicy to operate it in Germany as we are infermed
has been done since May of this year. However, ascording to eur
understanding, none of the soda ash produeed has been experted to
the United States. It is presumably distributed by the military
authorities in conformity with recommendations of the Emergeney

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.99)
LIBREY-OWENS-FORD GLASS CONPANY
Toledo 3, Ohie
-2-
Beonemie Committee for Europe with headquarters in Lendon. We wrge
that the proper authorities be persuaded to permit expertatiem ef
some of this German soda ash to the United States so leng as the
critieal needs exists here.
2)
Insofar as it is practicable, exports of soda ash from Japan mhould
be made to the United States at the earliest possible moment.
While it may ultimately be determined, in accordance with our
demilitarization policy, to move or dismantle some Japanese soda ash
plants, this certainly should not be done until the immediate needs
for soda ash in the United States are met.
There will doubtless be many practical and technical difficulties
involved, ranging all the way from obtaining necessary raw materials,
such as salt, to problems involved in restoring or reconditioning
and operating the soda ash plants in Japan. However, the problem
of getting soda ash to the United States for essential production
is so extremely important that surely means can be found to overcome
such difficulties, as was done in wartime.
3)
The most immediate possibility seems to be in Italy. We are informed
that the soda ash plant at Monfalcone can inerease its produetion
if coal can be obtained in adequate quantities. We have been asmured
that if additional coal is made available over the present alloeations
to Italy, the inereased production of soda ash will be made available
for use in the United States.
The Libbey.Owens.Ford Glass Company is willing to exeeute any
reasonable arrangement by which it'proeures and ships the coal,
with the approval of the United States Govermment, in exehange for
the exoess soda ash produced.
The sericusness of this situation is such a threat to America's whole
reconversien program that I am prepared to coeperate with you in any
way within my power to aid in carrying out these or other suggestions
necessary or appropriate to increase the available supply of soda ash
for use in the United States during this critical period when maximum
balanced production is so vital to our economy.
Sincerely,

PAPER: HC STER GARDLAN

~8 DEC 1946
ANOTHER POTSDAM?
The proposals for the future of
Japanese industry made by Mr.
Pauley, the United States Reparations'
Commissioner, were published almost
a month ago. The British attitude to
them ought to be made clear. Sir
Stafford Cripps has stated the prin-
ciples of British policy. Industries of
military importance should be re-
stricted, but others should be left
alone so that Japan might export
enough to become "internationally
solvent." But Sir Stafford said
nothing about the amount of restric-
tion which the Government con-
sidered necessary-or, in other words,
how great a transformation should be
demanded of Japanese industry. For
if some industries are cut out others
must be expanded.  Mr. Pauley's
plan demands an enormous change-
over, and at the same time removes
many of the resources needed to carry
it out. Besides war factories Japan
will lose all magnesium, aluminium,
celluloid, and synthetic oil plants, two-
thirds of her soda ash plant, and 95
per cent of her nitric acid plant.
Furnaces making 9,000,000 tons of
steel a year will be removed, leaving
Japan's iron and steel production
limited to 2,750,000 tons a year. This
is about one-fiftieth of the output of
the United States. Over two-thirds of
Japan's 600,000 machine tools will be
taken,, and in future only 10,000 new
tools a year can be made. Japan's
merchant navy will be limited to
1,500,000 tons—about one-third of its
pre-war size and smaller than it was
in 1914. The 250,000 tons of this
shipping which will be allowed to
trade outside the Japanese islands
will be confined to China, Korea,
Formosa, and Dairen. No vessel will
be larger than 5,000 tons or faster
than twelve knots. But perhaps the
most severe restriction of all is that
electricity generating plant will be
cut by at least one-half.
Everyone agrees that the manufac-
ture of armaments in Japan must be
forbidden and restrictions put on the
size of some potential armament
industries. Certainly some industrial
equipment would be a suitable kind
of reparations. Much of Japan's
heavy industry has been built up
since the early thirties for military
purposes. She could reasonably be
forced to abandon some of this
production and go beck to asgreater
dépendence  on

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.99)
1,500,000 tona-about one-third of its
pre-war size and smaller than it was
in 1914. The 250,000 tons of this
shipping which will be allowed to
trade outside the Japanese islands
will be confined to China, Korea,
Formosa, and Dairen. No yessel will
be larger than 5,000 tons or faster
than twelve knots. But perhaps the
most severe restriction of all is that
electricity generating plant will be
cut by at least one-half.
Everyone agrees that the manufac-
ture of armaments in Japan must be
forbidden and restrictions put on the
size of some potential armament
industries. Certainly some industrial
equipment would be a suitable kind
of reparations. Much of Japan's
heavy industry has been built up
since the early thirties for military
purposes. She could reasonably be
forced to abandon some of this
production and go back to agreater
dependence on textiles and light
industries. But Mr. Pauley's plan
takes too much and imposes too
drastic restrictions. Stripped of so
much of her industrial resources.
Japan could not quickly find other
jobs for her workers. She could not
export enough to pay for the imports
on which the standard of living—low
as it is—of 80,000,000 people depends.
Japan would become an economic
slum and wages would be relatively
even lower than they were before the
war.
The policy would be an error
comparable to what is being done in
Germany. It would not affect Japan
alone. Other Eastern countries would
lose both a market for their
commodities and the supplies of
manufactured goods which Japan
used to produce at prices they could
afford.
However crippled, the
Japanese would try,very hard to
balance their losses by large and
quick increases in their output of
consumer goods, such as textiles. Any
success they had would mean further
changes in the trade and industry of
other exporters, particularly Britain.
In short, Mr. Pauley's plan would
penalise many countries. It has not
yet been made the official American
policy, and it may not be. General
MacArthur and his officials are
probably opposing it. They are
trying to get some Japanese industries
going. But even the more construc-
tive American policies seem to be
aimed mainly at creating a Japanese
economy which will offer most
markets and least competition to
American industry. The British
Government should make its views
plain. In their own interests, as
well as those of other countries, the
Americans must be permaded that
they cannot make world trade larger
and freer if at the same-time' they
create more payments problems.
This they seem likelg to do in Japan.

sUBJECT: Telephone notes for discussion of SWNCC 236/37 (War Department
proposed amendments to SWNCC 236/33, Advance Transfers of
Japanese Reparations).
1. Paragraph d.c. cf the War Department's proposed amend-
ments is unacceptable to the State Department for the following
reasons:
a. SCAP's position as stated is not clear; the re-
commendations contained in C0MIN 3566 December are
vague and in conflict with statements in the later
COMIN 37 January.
b. The recommendations of this paragraph refer to
the assured retention level of industrial capacity
in Japan end to industrial disarmament. Issuance
of the directives on dvancedTransfers and Procedures
for Transfers in no way prejudice any decision the
United States may wish to make on these subjects
after SCAP's position is clarified.
c. Part d. of C0MIN 3566 refers to a program of
disarmament not received in Vashington upon which
comment, much less agreement, cannot be given.
2. Other than paragraph d.c. difference between State and
War Departments are editorial and can be worked out without much
difficulty.

My dear Kr. Secretary:
In furtherance of cur ocaupation objectives, General Maearther
has prompted the Japanese Covarnment to take stepe to impreve the
lot of the rural population and to reduce their caltaral backward-
ness. Legislation has been passed to provide a more equitable
distribution of agricultural land and to remove the virtual bend-
age of many tenant farmers to their landlerds. As other parts of
this general program of agriaultural reform, it is Americm poliay
to stimulste the growth of cooperative institutins among the rural
population and to encourage tax reform.
The State and War Departmente wish to assist the Sapreme
Coxmander as much as they can in his efforts to encourage reforms
affecting the agriaultural population. as one way of doing so, it
is planned to supply for temporary duty with the Natural Besouroes
Section of SCap's staff several specialists who can sorve as ad-
visors and who can give some supervision to the exeoution of lame
the Japanese have passed. After consultation with General Macarthar
about his requirements, the War Department proposes to send to Japan
894.6TA
two specialists to deal with problems of land tenure and teneney!
one specialist on agrioultural financs, particularly the reorganise-
tion of credit institutions and tax problems, and one specialist to
aid in the expansion and democratisation of rural cooperatives and
other farm groups. Inasmuch as the Japanese have enacted legiala-
tion on land redistribution and tenancy, the specialists on these
subjects are needed to supervise the execution of the formalated
plans and to advise upon the administration of the programs. The
specialists on the other subjects should be individials with polioy
and planning experience as they will be required as advisors on
prograes being developed.
8

-1347
assistance of the Department of Agriculture in the selection of
the four specialists mentioned above. We desire to obtain as
quickly as possible the nanes, with brief statements of qualifica-
tions, of persons who would be in a position to take these
assignments

FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
2516 MASSACHUSETTS AVENUE, N. W.
WASHINGTON 8, D. C.
20 February 1947
surlec
The Honorable George C. Marahall
Secretary of State
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Secretary:
The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission provide
that one of the functions of the Commission should be to "formulate the
policies, principles and standards in conformity with which the fulfill-
ment by Japan of its obligations under the Terms of Surrender may be
accomplished."
It is further provided that when such decisions are made by the
894.60/2-2047
Far Eastern Commission, "The United States Government shall prepare
directives in accordance with the policy decisions of the Commission and
shall transmit them to the Supreme Commander through the appropriate
United States Government agency."
At the forty-sixth meeting of the Far Eastern :Commission held
at 25l6 Massachusetts Avenue, Northwest, Washington, D. C., on 20 Febru-
ary l947, the enclosed policy decision relative to Assured Production
Capacity Levels for Japan was unanimously approved.
As Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I have been
instructed to forward this decision to you on behalf of the Commission,
in order that the appropriate directives may be prepared and transmitted
to the Supreme Commander in accordance with the Terms of Reference.
Before the enclosed policy decision was formally approved by the
Commission, the Chinese Representative made a statement setting forth the
position of his Government with regard to paragraph 5, Iron and Steel
Industry, of the enclosed decision. He recommended that his statement be
recorded in the minutes and be transmitted to the Supreme Commander for
his information. Excerpt from the draft minutes of the Commission's
forty-sixth meeting, including this statement verbatim, is enclosed. The
French Representative expressed his concurrence in the Chinese statement.
The Commission has accordingly instructed me to ask that the United States
Government in transmitting the appropriate directive based on the enclosed
policy decision to the Supreme Commander, also forward the Chinese state-
ment for the Supreme Commander's information.
In adopting the enclosed policy decision, the Commission accepted
the recommendation of the Supreme Commander that the decision not be re
AR01947
FILED

=== Page 456 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
leased to the press until specific plants had been designated by the
Supreme Commander pursuant to the terms of the enclosed policy. The
Commission would appreciate it if the Supreme Commander could inform
us when in his opinion the release of this policy decision to the press
would be appropriate.
The Australian Representative pointed out that consultation
with the Allied Council, as provided by the enclosed policy decision,
might involve public disclosure of its contents and desired that it
be understood that a Power would not be violating the Commission's
decision on publication if it raised any question connected with the
policy at a meeting of the Allied Council.
Sincerely yours,
Nelom methuow
Nelson T. Johnson
Secretary General

ENCLOSURE
Prabl Pereeler. Kelaluve te
ASSURED PRODUCTION CAPACITY LEVELS FOR JAPAN
1. Interim assured production capacity levels are hereby established
for each of the eight categories of Japanese industry listed below.
The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, upon consultation
with the Allied Council for Japan in accordance with the Terms of Reference
of the Allied Council for Japan, may designate for retention specific plants
and establishments, associated with each category, whose aggregate produc-
tion capacity does not exceed the level established for that category.
Such designation should be interpreted to mean that:
a. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, upon consultation
with the Allied Council for Japan in accordance with the Terms of Refer-
ence of the Allied Council for Japan, decides that operation of these
plants and establishments, individually and collectively, is practical
in terms of current needs for their products, and in terms of suoh con-
ditioning factors as availability of raw materials, power, and labor;
b. owners and management are assured that the particular facility
will not be subject to removal under the Interim Program or subsequent
disarmament or reparations programs.
2.
Machine Tool Industry
The assured production caracity level for Japan's machine tool
industry should be placei at a balanced type-size aggregate of 7,500 units
ennually.
3.
Arsenals, Army nd Navy
0
No assured production capacity level should be assigned to Army and
Navy arsenals.
N
2047
4.
Aircraft Industry (including aircraft engines)
No assured production capecity level should be assigned to the
aircraft industry.
5.
Iron and Steel Inductry
The assured production capacity level for Japan's iron and steel
industry should be placed at an aggregate of 2.0 million metric tons of
steel ingot, and an aggregate of 800,000 metric tons of pig iron annually.
6.
Light Metals Industry
No assured production capacity for Japan's light metals industry
should be given.

FEC-083/5
-1-
NND.780069
ByTHGIMKW NARS, Date 7/30/79

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.98)
-OONPIDENTIAL
2
7. Thermal Electric Power Industry
The assured production capacity level for Japan's thermal
electric power industry should be placed at an aggregate installed public
utility capacity of l.O million kilowatts.
8.
Soda Ash, Chlorine. end Caustic Soda Industry
The assured production capacity level for Japan's soda ash,
chlorine, and caustic soda industry should be placed at 207,000 metric tons
of soda ash, of which 72,000 are required to produce caustic soda, 28,000
metric tons of chlcrine, and 80,000 metric tons of caustic soda annually.
9.
Sulphuric Acid Industry
The assured production capacity level for the Japanese sulphurio
acid industry should be placed at 3,000,000 metric tons annually.

EXCERPT FROM THE MINUTES OF THE FORTY-SIXTH MEETING OF THE FEC, 2O FEB. 1947
ITEM 3 - ASSURED PRODUCTION CAPACITY LEVEL FOR JAPAN
(FEC-083/2,FEC-083/3, FEC-083/4)
中
(STATEMENT BY THE CHINESE MEMBER)
Dr. Ko0 presented the following formal statement:
"In keeping with the constant desire of the Chinese Government to
promote cooperation in the Far Eastern Commission and expedite its work,
I am prepared to vote for the paper, although with some reluctance. I
say with some reluctance because we feel that with particular regard to
the capacity level for Japan's steel ingot and pig iron industry, to
assure an aggregate of 2 million metric tons of steel ingot and an
aggregate of 800,000 metric tons of pig iron annually is premature at
the present time and also based on too nigh an estimate. On the basis
of the latest information from SCAP (Summation of Non-Military Activities
in Japan, No. 13 , October 1946, p. 91) the production of steel ingot and
castings in October 1946 reached only the level of 60,000 metric tons
and that of pig iron only 10,Oo0 metric tons. We therefore doubt the
urgency to decide upon an assured capacity for Japan which would provide
for a production greatly in excess of the present production.
"I want to point out further that as late as l93l, the year Japan
invaded Manchuria, the total Japanese steel consumption was only about
2 million metric tons. Even in that year there was reason to believe
that a substantial part of the consumption was for war production or
for shipbuilding and expansion of capital facilities. None of the above
needs to be assured for Japan's rehabilitation should be considered as
having an urgent priority over reparations to severely damaged Allied
nations.
"Therefore, I wish to make it clear that the Chinese Government will
accept this policy paper with the express understanding that it does not
preclude the possibility of using the increased current production from
such assured capacity to furnish a part of the reparations.
"I hope you will allow this to be recorded in the minutes and com-
municated along with the policy paper to SCAP."
MR. NAGGIAR (French Representative) said that he desired to record his
Government's approval of the position just expressed by Dr. Koo.
GENERAL HILLDRING said that he had no objection to the record containing
this statement of understanding on the part of the Chinese representative.
He desired to point out, however, that the subject of reparations from current
production had not previously been considered by the Commission and he wanted
to make it clear that the United States Government could not at this time com-
mit itself on any proposal involving reparations from current production. DR.
Ko0 said that the understanding he had just recorded naturally applied only to
the Chinese Government and that he simply wished to reserve the right of the
Chinese Government to raise the question at the appropriate time.

BRITISH INDUSTRIAL PROPERTIES: KOBE
Messrs. J. & P. Coats. Ltd.
T.W.E.D. Ref. P/30545
S.C.A.P. Ref. 05274
On 17th December 1946 I visited the factory
(formerly of Teikoku Seishi) of Fuji Boseki K.K. at
Yasunaka, Naka-kawachigori, Osaka-fu.
2.
It is my understanding that Messrs. J. & P.
Coats held a financial.interest in the Teikoku Seishi
K.K. prior to the war. During the war an amalgamation
was effected with the Fuji Boseki K.K. and in July 1943
this factory was named the Hachio or Yao factory of
Fuji Boseki K.K., manufacturers of cotton yarns. The
following business executives were met-
Mr. M. Saito
Business Manager
Mr. S. Honda
Works Manager
Mr. Y. Aizawa
Asst. Works Manager
Mr. C. Yamamoto
Production Department
3.
I was informed that during 1943 or 1944 all
fine spindles, consisting of 33 sets or 14,700 spindles,
were removed under orders of the Japanese Government to
be converted into scrap. The management had no knowl-
edge as to whether this material was actually converted
into scrap or whether it is still available elsewhere,
but so far as Fuji Boseki K.K. was concerned the
machines were handed over to the Government authorities
and they were unable to state what happened to them.
The entire factory was in no way damaged during the
war. The cotton yarn spinning machines at present in
use were stated to consist of 29,242 spindles together
with auxiliary machines. A complete inventory of the
factory is being taken and a detailed list will be
available at a later date.
4.
The number of workers at present consist of
512 in the factory and 43 in the office - 555 in all.
The present work schedule is 48-hours a week with no
night shift. The present production of machine thread
was stated to be 3,300 pounds per 8-hour day, it being
said that production has been maintained at this level
for some time. Very few changes had been made in the
factory since prewar days although some improvements
had been made in the sanitary arrangements.
5.
The two large bays in which the fine spindles
had been

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. to Tokyo's
907, March 12,
1947.
- 2 -
had been installed were at present used for the storage
of a considerable amount of machinery which had been
brought from other factories operated by the Fuji Boseki.
It is hoped to obtain an inventory of this stock later.
All buildings were in an excellent state of preservation.
Proper attention had been given to the paintwork and
roofs were being kept watertight.
6.
I was informed that a Mr. Curtiss, previously
connected with Messrs. J. & P. Coats, had visited this
factory in October last year when he was in the area with
the Australian forces. The management were fully aware
of their responsibility in looking after the Allied
interest in their concern.
7.
No custodian appears to have been appointed
for Messrs. J. & P. Coats' interest.
8.
The address of Messrs. J. & P. Coats in the
United Kingdom is 155 St. Vincent Street, Glasgow, so
far as is known here.
H.C. LEPPER.A.M.I. Meoh.B.

Dear General Wickersham:
I am happy to say that a cable has just bees received from
Tokye giving information concerning the Teikoku Seishi Kabushiki
Kaisha. The text of the cable is as follows:
"Teikoku Seishi Kabushiki Kaisha was dissolved and
amalgamated by the Fuji Spinning Company, Limited,
in July 1943. The Puji Spiming Cempany 1s presently
operating and in exoellent condition and was im no
way damaged during the war.
"The United Kimgden liaison missien im Japan has been
advised that SCAP is protecting the interests in
Japan of Messrs J and P Ceates Company as those of a
United Nations national. The plant has not been
taken under physieal custody of the occupatien ferces
but the Japanese Gevernment has been dirseted to
preserve and protect the property."
If more details are needed I shall be glad to pursue the
matter further.

DIRECTIVE TO THE SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
ASSURED PRODUCTION CAPACITY LEVELS FOR JAPAN
The following diroctive, prepared by the State Department
to implement the policy adopted by the Far Eastern Comission on
February 20, 1947, under the provisions of paragraph II, A, 1, or
its terms of reference has been received fron the State, War
and Navy Departnents for transmission to you for your guidanco
in accordance with paragraph III, 1, of those tcrms of reference:
"1. Interim assured production capacity levels are hereby
established for each of the eight categories of Japanese
industry listed below.
The Supreme Coimander for the Allied Powere, upon consulta-
tion with the Allicd Council for Japan in accordance with the
terms of reference of the Allied Council for Japan, may
designate for retention specific plants and establishments,
associatcd with cach category, whose aggregate production
capacity does not exceed the level established for that cate-
gory. Such designation should be interpreted to mean that:
2. The Supreme Conmander for the Allied Powers, upon
consultation with the Allied Council for Japan in accord-
ance with the terms of reference of the Allied Council for
Japan, decides that operation of these plants and establish-
monts, individually and collectively, is practical in torms
of current needs for their products, end in terms of such
conditioning factors as availability of raw materials,
power, and labor;

=== Page 475 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
b. Owners and management are assured that the
facility will not be subject to renoval under the
program or subsequent disarmament or reparations prs
"2. Machine tool industry
The assured production capacity level for Japan's maoh
tool industry should be placed at a balanced type-size argr
gate of 7,500 units annually.
"3. Arsenals, Army and Navy
No assured production capacity level should be assigned
to Army and Navy arsenals.
"4. Aircraft industry (including aircraft engines)
No assured production capacity level should be assigned
to the aircraft industry.
"5.
Iron and steel industry
The assured production capacity level for Japan's iron
and steel industry should be placed at an aggregate of 2.0
million metric tons of steel ingot, and an aggregate of
800, 000 metric tons of pic iron annually.
"6.
Light metals industry
No assured production capacity for Japan's light metals
industry should be given.
"7.
Thermal electric power industry
The assured production capacity level for Japan's thernal
electric power industry should be placed at an aggregate
installed public utility capacity of 1.0 million kilowatts.
"8.
Soda ash, chlorine, and caustic soda industry
The assured production capacity level for Japan s soda ash,
chlorine, and caustic soda industry should be pleced at 207,000
metric tons of soda ash, of which 72, 000 are required to pro
duce caustic soda, 28,000 metric tons of chlorine, and 60,000
metric tons of caustic soda annually.
"9.
Sulphuric acid industry
The assured production capacity level for the Japanese sal-
phuric acid industry should be placed at 3,000,000 metric
tons annually."

and substitute paragraph therefor; and the addition of
paragraph 41.

This addition, however, was inserted as the penultimate
sentence in paragraph 7 of the Discussion (Appendix "B").

the attached revised pages 315 through 319 - A and pages 335
and 336 for the ones contained therein and destroy the super
seded pages by burning.

SECRET
ENCLOSURE
U.S. POLICY ON REPARATIONS ALLOCATIONS PROCEDURES FOR
INDUSTRIAL FACILITIES IN JAPAN
THE PROBLEM
1. To determine U. S. policy with respect to the procedures
pertaining to the allocation, selection, accounting, and certain
other administrative matters concerning handling of specific
items of industrial facilities to be removed from Japan as
reparations.
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. See Apperdix "A".
DISCUSSION
3. See Appendix "B".
CONCLUSIONS
4. It is concluded that the following procedures govern
SCAP implementation of those aspects of the Japanese reparations
program providing for the removal from Japan of industrial
facilities and equipmert:
a. In accordance with provisions of directives to SCAP
regarding availability for reparations transfer of categor-
ies of Japanese industry, SCAP should be responsible for
final selection of those particular industrial facilities
and equipment to be made available for removal from apan
as reparations.
b. SCAP should be responsible for preparing an
inventory of individual Japanese industrial facilities
selected under a above. This inventory shall inolude any
available information which would aid a prospective claimant
in determining the utility of the individual facility,
such as technical specifications, make, age and condition.
c. SCAP should be responsible for assigning monetary
valuation to industrial facilities selected and listed under
a and b. Procedures for uniform monetary valuation should
Enclosure
315
SWNCC 236/35
(Reviaed 17 March 1947)

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.99)
be determined by SCAP. Valuations should
detail to permit charges to be made for partao
which may be allocated separately. Values assign
reparations assets referred to in a and b above need
no relation to any other values as long as these valu
consistent among the reparations assets comprised or
industrial facilities and equipment.
a. SCAP should submit to reparations claimant countr
the inventory provided in b, and the valuation provided
in c.
e. Upon receipt of directives indicating the over-all
shares assigned to each country, SCAp should set up
appropriate reparations accounts for recipient countries,
The value of the facilities received by any one country
should not exceed its percentage share of the total value
of the industrial facilities selected and valued by SCAP
for removal as reparations. As provided in the "Deliveries
of Reparations Goods in Japan", SWNCC 236/27, a recipient
country will make a commitment to accept a specific
facility at the time allocation is made. SCaP should
likewise agree to reserve the accepted facility for the
claimant country. At such time SCaP will make an initial
chargc of the monetary value assigned to the facility
(in accordance with 4 c above) to the reparations account
of the recipient country. When final title is taken by
a reoipient country to an individual facility aboard ship,
a final charge shall be made against the reparations
account of the recipient country, taking intc account, if
necessary, any unforeseen loss of value.
f. From those items of industrial facilities and equip-
ment declared availahle for reparations removals SCAP
should allocate particular industrial facilities to each
country on the basis of requests submitted by that country.
To the extent that requests of two or more countries are
in conflict, he will work out what seems to him the most
Enclosure
-316
SWNCC 236/35
(Revised 17 March 1947)

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
reasonable allocation, taking into primary consideration
the portion of each country's allocation still unfilled.
After taking into account this primary consideration and
other things being equal, consideration should be given
the following four items in stated order of priority:
(1) requests for whole plants and related faoili-
ties, as opposed to requests for portions thereof.
(2) requests for parts of plants or related groups
of items which can be integrated with the existing
industrial pattern of the requesting country.
(3) the extent to which the requested item or items
would replace property which was destroyed, damaged,
or looted in the war, or requires replacement because
of excessive wear incident to prosecuting the war
against Japan.
(4) the extent to which the requesting country
depended in pre-war years on imports from Japan of
the item or items (or products thereof) requested.
g. In making allocations as provided by paragraph f,
SCAP should, to the extent practicable, give consideration
to striking a fair and equitable balance as between all
requesting countries of items:
(1) in integrated or consolidated units.
(2) of modern and efficient design and manufacture.
(3) in good working condition.
h. Each country entitled to receive an allocation of
industrial equipment from Japan on reparations.account
chould be permitted to have a technical mission in Japan.
These miocions should be affordcd full opportunity for
inspection of plants declared available. They should be
authorized by their governments to make commitments to
accept title to plants selected, to aocept final title to
industrial facilities on reparations account, and to make
all necessary arrangements with SCAP including scheduling
SWNCC 236/35
317-
Enc losure
(Revised 17 March 1947)

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.99)
S E C R E T
the required shipping for the actual removal of allooated
facilities in accordance with the principles laid down in
"Deliveries of Reparations Goods in Japan" (SWNCc 236/27).
1. SCAP should organize a Reparations Technical
Advisory Committee (RTAc) composed of the heads of the
Reparations Technical Missions located in Japan and chaired
by a Representative of SCAP appointed by SCAP. The function
of this Committee should be to assist SCAp, in an advisory
capacity, in the development of technical and administra-
tive procedures to assure an orderly removal of reparations
goods from Japan, and in the settlement of conflicts
between member countries arising over requests for parti-
cular facilities. In the event of disagreement between
SCAP and member nations concerning the allocation of a
particular facility by SCAP, if a formal protest 1s made
to SCAP by two-thirds of the members of RTAC, SCAP who
has final authority in such matters may, in his discretion,
refer the dispute to the U.S, Government.
j. Countries receiving industrial machinery, equip-
ment, or supplies from Japan on reparations account should
obligate themselves not to offer such assets for sale or
barter to another country within three years from the date
of assignment of title of facilities, (as defined in SWNCq
236/27) except as such facilities may be bartered among
reparations receiving countries in exchange for other
assets allotted on reparations account. In so far as ful~
fillment of the U.S. claim for industrial equipment is in
excess of ability or desire of the U.S., to utilize it, the
U.S. shall have the right to allocate or otherwise dispose
of its claim to other claimants and to Korea on a political
basis. Claimant countries should be permitted to dispose
of reparations goods to their own nationals, wherever
domiciled and for use anywhere except Japan; to any person
of non-enemy nationality for use within the recipient
country; and to satisfy private claims arising out of war
damage.

=== Page 485 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
k. Claims for individual facilities should be pre-
sented to SCAP by the representatives of claimant countries
2
in Japan within six months after the submisaion by SCAp to
claimant countries of the inventory and evaluation of such
facility. Each claimant will arrange for transportation for
its reparations goods at the port and will be prepared to
accept transfer of title no later than two years after the
designation of the goods for application to that country'a
reparations account, providing that such goods have been
delivered to the loading point at the port within that
period. Industrial facilities, machinery, or equipment
(other than specialized war making equipment) declared
available for reparations, but either (1) unrequested for
application against recognized reparations claims on
Japanese assets from within Japan or, (2) undelivered
within time limits set herein should not be destroyed or
otherwise disposed of by SCAP pending receipt of further
directives.
5. It is further conoluded that the provisions of para-
graph 4 c and d requiring a monetary valuation for industrial
facilities shall not operate to delay delivery of goods under
"Advance Transfers of Japanese Reparations." The provisions of
paragraph 4 f and the provisions of that part of paragraph 41
which permit recipient countries to barter goods reooived on
reparations account among themselves and whioh permit the U.S.
to dispose of its claim to other countries on a politioal basis
do not apply to the program of "Advance Transfers of Japanese
Reparations." As soon as recognized and approved national repara-
tions shares have been determined and announoed by direotive to
SCAP all of the provisions of this policy statement will govern
and provisions of "Advance Transfers of Japanese Reparations"
will be rendered inoperative.

a. The State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee, after

to SCAP at the same time that the Conclusions of SWNCC

c. No parts of this document be made available for.

APPENDIX "A"
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. Appendix, SWNCC 236/27, Delivery of Reparations Goods
In Japan.
"1. In delivering reparations goods to claimant
countries, the Japanese Government should bear all costs
connected with dismantling, packing, transporting to a
port in Japan for waterborne craft or for airborne craft
designated by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
handling, and loading on board the craft at that port.
None of these costs should be charged to the recipient
country's reparations account.
"2. The goods in question should become the property
of the recipient country when they have been delivered on
board at the designated port and, thereafter, they become
the entire responsibility of such recipient country.
The
goods in question should be charged to the reparations
account of the recipient country not later than the time
at which they have been delivered on board at the designated
port.
"3. When particular reparations goods have been
designated for application to the reparations account of
a particular country, the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers may, before procecding with the removal of such
goods from their location at the time of such designation,
require that country to provide him with a written under-
taking to accept such goods in accordance with the pro-
visions of this paper, provided they are delivered on board
at the designated port free from any major damage sustained
in processes of dismantling, transportation, and loading.
"4. a. Each claimant nation will be responsible for
furnishing the waterborn or airborne craft necessary
to transport its reparations goods from the designated
port in Japan.

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
b. In accordance with the provisions of this paper,
each claimant nation will furnish transportation for its
reparations goods at the port and will be prepared to
accept transfer of title no later than two years after
the designation of the goods for application to that
country's reparations account, provided that such goods
have been delivered to the loading point at the port
within that period. Otherwise, the goods will revert
to SCAP for allocation to another claimant or for dis-
position under the provisions of other directives.
c. For purposes of developing priorities and
schedules for dismantling operations and removal of
reparations goods to the port, each claimant nation
will notify SCAP of the order desired for receiving
its respective reparations goods and the prospective
availability of shipping to move these goods from the
port. Firm schedules for dismantling operations,
removal to the port, and loading on board recipient's
craft will be determined by the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers in agreement with the representatives
of the recipient country."

"APPENDIX 'D'
"THE COMMENTS OF THE
SCAP ON AMBASSADOR
PAULEY'S
EPORT
"Paraphrase of telegram Cm-In-4219, Tokyo, February 18,
1946:
Thig is in reference to your W 94752. Part I of IV.
It is recommended that Washington directives on
reparations removals should indicate proportion of capacity
of industry and leave to this Headquarters discretion in the
matter of which reparations are to be chosen and when they
are to be removed.
Shipping, transportation and like
economic considerations could best be served in that manner.
Industry in Japan has not as yet been evaluated in relation
to the countryis economy, and there has been no clear
indication as tc what Allied policy on Japan's economic
future will be.
If purposes cf occupation are to be best
served it is recessary that the reparations removal program
be characterized by the greatest flexibility . . . . ."
"Part II
We are now going ahead with prugram for tentative
inspection and listing of equipment to be removed.
This 1s
in compliance with your W 94752. This equipment to be
classified as to its condition, and whether or not it will
be available for removal. . ."

possible disruption of approved industrial programs being

7.
Delivery of Reparations Goods in Japan. (SWNCC 236/20),
page 244.
"In delivering reparations goods to claimant countries,
the Jananese Government should bear all costs connected
with dismantling, packing, transporting to a port in Japan
designated by the Supreme Commander, handling, and loading
on board at that port. None of these costs should be
charged to the recipient country's reparati ns account.
"The goods in question should become the property of
the recipient country and be charged to its reparations
account only when they have been delivered on board at the
designated port and thereafter they become the entire
responsibility of such recipient country.
"When particular reparations goods have been designated
for application to the reparations account of a particular
country, the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers may,
before proceeding with the removal of such goods from the
site, require that country to provide him with a written
undertaking to accept such goods provided they are delivered
on board in good condition at the designated port in accord-
ance with the foregoing."
