C RE T
APPENDIX
"B"
DISCUSSION
1.
The United States Government should attempt to obtain
inter-Allied agreement regarding the responsibilities to be as-
signed to the Supreme Commander for the implementation of the
reparations settlement, the functions to be performed in Japan by
representatives of claimant countries, broad treatment of the
valuation of assets declared available for transfer and the pro-
cedure to be used in matching the value of requested items
against recognized claims. Agreement should be established as
to the time limit within which execution of the settlement will
be completed and the limitations to be imposed upon governmental
disposal of assets which have been received.
The conclusions em-
bodied in paragraph 4 represent the views of
the United States
Government.
Allied agreement in
those views
is
advisable if the
reparations program for Japan is
to proceed with the least dis-
ruption of political relations in the Pacific area as a whole and
to compensate for damages due to war effort in such a way as to
encourage prompt economic rchabilitation.
2.
It is recognized that the Far Eastern Commission should
formulate those policy docisions required in determining what is
necessary for
the Japanese pcacctime economy
and what general
oategories of goods are available for removal or
destruction.
Howev r, it is asserted once more that he Supreme Commander
shoula have the authority to select particular industrial facili-
ties removable as reparations within broad categories of industry
declared surplus to Japan's pcacctime needs.
The Supreme Gom
mander's authority to make such selections should be final.
Only
he possesses that intimate knowledge of the structure and func-
tioning of the Japanese
economy nccessary to determine what can
be removed without preventing cfrective uso of over-all
capacities determined for retention.
SWNCC 236/35
-332 -
Appendix "B"
SECRET

=== Page 500 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
3.
Only the Supreme Commander can compile the results of
his determination of what is to be listed for actual removal as
reparations.
He should be responsible for preparing an inventory
of selected industrial facilities and for making that inventory
available to reparations claimants as promptly as practicable.
It is to
the
interest of the Supreme Commander to make such an
inventory as
complete, detailed and accurate as possible, since
defects ana deficiencies will undoubtedly lead to requests by
claimant countries to amplify their knowledge of listed assets
which they might be interested in requesting. Adequate initial
infornation will minimize servicing of claimant countries dis-
satisfied with fragmentery or
erroneous entries in the inventory.
4.
The Supreme Commander should assign monetary valuation
to industrial facilities listed in his inventory.
The conclusions
above do not indicate procedures wich he must use for this pur-
pose beyond stating that valuations should be detailed and uniform.
They should be detailed so that reyuests for single items, as
well as integrated and
consolidated facilities, may be valued.
They shoula be uniform
so that risks of seeming discrimination
or preferential treatment towards requesting governments by the
Supreme Commander can be kept at a minimum.
It is recognized that
whatever method of
monetary valuation is adopted, there will be
complaints that
it
does not
correspon
to values which the re-
ceiving country would place upon the same item.
Costs of trans-
portation, differences of local conditions in the absorbing
country, age, condition of equipment, etc., will effect actual
value to the requesting country and may introduce factors render-
ing the Supreme Commander's valuation imperfect.
However, if, for
example, a pre-war yen book value, adjusted for depreciation and
war damage, is given to all available facilities, a certain con-
sistency, comparability and uniformity will have been established,
the importance of which would outweigh other procedures attempt-
ing to assign real and current value to the inventoried items.
SWNCC 236/35
- 333 -
Appendix "B"

=== Page 501 === (confidence 0.99)
S ECR ET
5.
It will be determined what countries are eligible to re-
ceive reparations and how much.
Each eligible country should be
authorized by the Supreme Commander to assign a mission to Japan
to handle technical reparations questions. These missions should
be afforded full opportunity to study SCAP's inventories, obtain
additional information needed from SCAP and if
necessary to inspect
plants which have been inventoried and valued.
To the extent that
they are
accorded hospitality and cooperation, they will be dis-
inclined to find fault with their dependent position relative to
the Supreme Commander.
By their own governments, these missions
should be authorized to make commitments to accept title at the
time allocation of facilities is made, and to accept delivery of
requested industrial facilities when delivered aboard ship or air
transport, and to make all necessary practical arrangements with
the Supreme Commander and their
own governments for actual re-
moval of allocated facilities.
The costs of dismantling, repair-
ing, maintaining and transporting requested facilities within Japan
will
be borne by the Japanese Government.
At dockside, the
claimant country would take final possession of the asset and be
responsible for bearing all costs, transportation, installation,
etc., outside of Japan.
No industrial asset available for repara-
tions would be dismantled
or moved about within Japan unless a
claimant country had made
a clear
commitment to take final title.
Missions in Japan should be expected to stand ready to
assist
the Supreme
Commander in the exercise of what must be ac-
cepted as his final authority to allocate particular facilities.
At the Supreme Commander's request, each mission would participate
in selection of particular facilities suitable particularly for
use
or
operation within its own country.
The Supreme Commander
should insist that these activities be performed only by fully
authorized representatives of claimant governments.
(If tech-
nicians, engineers, economists, or other specialists are sent to
Japan, they must in all cases operate as governmental representa-
tives and not as agents of any private interest.)
SWNCC
236/35
- 334 -
Appendix "B"

=== Page 502 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
6.
The Supreme Commander will desire the assistanoe of an
inter-Allied technical agency in working on aspects of repara-
tions which could be interpreted as lying neither in the field
of pure policy nor pure implementation. It is contemplated that
he should appoint a chairman to lead such a group and should
expect that group to cooperate with him in the coordination among
the various interested countries, of uniform technical and admin-
1strative procedures, in the settlement of conflicting requests
for particular facilities and in the adjustment of other problems
connected with
the
execution of the reparations program.
It is
considered that
the
Supreme Commander should himself determine the
terms of
reference under which this group would operate, (1) in
order to emphasize its technical
function as distinct from the
policy-making functions which it might desire to have and (2) in
order to make clear its subordination to
the Supreme Commander's
final authority.
It is recommended that
this group be oalled the
Reparations Technical Advisory Committee (RTAC).
Only
two specific
instructions are giver to the Supreme Commander with respect to
RTAC; first, RTAC should be
composed of
the heads of
the technical
reparations missions located in Japan; and second, if the RTAC
by a two-thirds vote makes a formal protest to SCAP regarding his
decision allooating a particular facility to a requesting claimant,
SCAP may at his discretion refer the dispute to
the U.S. Govern-
ment.
It is considered advisable
to permit SCAP
to refer
such
matters of
serius international disagreement
the U.S. Govern-
ment which
in
turn may refer
the problem to the
FEC
for authorita-
tive international decision.
These foregoing instructions should
in no way
be considered as limiting SCAp's authority to deaigrate
particular facilities for retention or removal, or to allocate such
facilities to particular claimants, but rather should be considered
as providing flexible administrative procedures for obtaining
settlement of disputes on which strong international disagreement
is evident and a shifting of responsibility to an authoritative
international body, appears advisable.
These rights are confined
to
these
two matters and could not be asserted as a challenge to
the Supreme Commander's authority in designating particular
facilities for retention or for removal.
SWNCC 236/35
335

=== Page 503 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
7.
The Supreme Commander will be instructed as to the h
of the industrial facilities within Japan, which he has deolared
available for allocation, to be allotted to each claimant country.
The Supreme Commander's allocation of faoilities to each olaimant
country should not exceed in value the assigned percentage share,
In fulfilling each claimant country's recognized claima, the
Supreme Commander will utilize formal requests submitted by olaim-
ant countries for types of industrial facilities or partioularly
specified industrial facilities and equipment. It may be assumed
that two or more countries will frequently request the same plant
or piece of equipment. When this occurs, the Supreme Commander
will work out what seems to him a reasonable decision in the light
of certain factors to be considered. The first and most important
of these factors is the size of a claimant country's unfulfilled
percentage share of the total pool of available industrial facili-
ties. Iritially, countries with large unfulfilled percentages will
be accorded preference over countries with small percentages; and,
as the program progresses, countries whose peroentages have been
proportionately more fully filled will be given less preference
than those whose percentages are proportionately less fully filled.
Other factors which the Supreme Commander should take into account
are, in general, size, workability and need (not in terms of the
claimant country's economy as a whole so much as in terms of the
actual effect of hostilities upon parts of that economy). Hence,
other things being equal, the Supreme Commander should give pre-
ference to requests for whole plants accompanied by facilities
integrated in or consolidated with them. For example, a request
for steel capacity accompanied by steel rolling mill, power,'trans.
portation and other equipment should be given preference over a
request for only one part of that industrial complex. The Supreme
Commander should give preference to requests for parts of plants or
related groups of items which can be integrated with an existing and
functioning industrial operation or program within the requesting
country. (Integration in this connection should be taken to mean
the ability of the recipient country to utilize these assets in the
production of goods and ser/ices rather than to use them as sorap
or not at all.) It is acsuned that reparations removals of in-
dustrial capacity offer greater prospect of practical economic util-
ization when they supplement existing capacity than when they cnstitute a completely

=== Page 504 === (confidence 0.99)
2
SECRET
new industrial operation.
Considerations of utilization within the
recipient country are appropriate only when supported by convincing
evidence submitted by the claimant country. If no such evidence
were presented by any of the claimant countries, the Supreme Com-
mander would not be expected to make an independent assessment of
the feasibility of integrating an item removed into an industrial
pattern already existing in the requesting country. The Supreme
Commander should, moreover, take account of evidence presented by
claimant countries that requested items would actually replace
property which was destroyed, damaged, or looted in the war, or
requires replacement because of excessive wear and tear in war pro-
duction.
Finally, the Supreme Commander should give preference to
requests made by claimant countries for industrial equipment which
figured prominently in producing their pre-war imports from Japan.
Some claimant countries will state that dependence upon Japan for
such imports constituted a threat to
their own internal economic
stability and in some
cases a menace to their political position.
Such countries should feel free to present evidence that removal
of certain items from Japan to their countries would minimize
unhealthy dependence of this sort.
8.
The Far Eastern Comnission has adopted a policy instruct-
ing the Supreme Commander to strike a fair balance between Japan's
own needs and the requests of reparations claimant countries for
particular items so far as quality of those items is concerned.
The Supreme Comnander should similarly be instructed to strike a
balance between conflicting and competing requests of claimant
countries amongst themselves.
All countries will desire facilities
in good working conditions, of modern and efficient design and
manufacture, and most countries will want consolidated and inte-
grated units.
The Supreme Commander should avoid giving prefer-
ential treatment to any single country in allocating the best
facilities. Since valuations appearing in the Supreme Commander's
inventory will in many cases imperfectly reflect real value, he
may have to exercise considerable discretion in assuring claimants

=== Page 505 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
that amongst items of equal book value he will distribute the
really good facilities on an equitable basis.
9.
The Supreme Commander will be wholly responsible for main-
tenance of
such records as are necessary to show how credits as-
signed to claimant countries, in accordance with FEC and/or the
Reparations Conference decisions of percentage shares, are expended
in payment for items from the total pool of industrial facilities
available for transfer. It is probable that there will be dis-
parity between industrial facilities declared available as
reparations anā facilities collectively requested by all repara-
tions claimants.
In short, some parts of total credits (corres-
ponding to percentage shares of the value of the total pool of
facilities avcilable for removal) will remain unexpended.
The
Supreme Comman er's record of such unexpended credits will, as
pointed out above, have a direct and in some
cases decisive
influence upon the preference he will show in allocating actually
requested items.
10.
To facilitate the work of the Supreme Commander in pre-
venting the assignment of facilities to any one country in excess
of its total recognized reparations claim and in applying to
conflicting claims the criteria of unfilled reparations claims
mentioned above the value of
an
individual facility as determined
by the Supreme Commander should
be provisionally charged to the
account of the recipient
nation at the time the facility is
selected and assigned to a particular country.
This charge is
tentative, ana is to be used only for the purposes indicated
above.
If, in the process of dismantling, packing and transport-
ing the facility, major damage occurs, sufficient to warrant
r jection of the facility by the recipient country, the charge
would be removed entirely. Lesser unforeseen damage may result
in a reduction of the initial charge in the final accounting.
At he time final
title is accepted by the recipient country,
when the facility is delivered aboard air or sea transport, a
final charge shall be entered against the claim of that country,
taking into
account
such unforeseen losses in value.

=== Page 506 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
11. It has been the view of the United States Government that
industrial facilities in Japan have very little negotiable value.
If there is any market for their cash sale, it is so limited and
so insusceptible of orderly organization that any attempt by the
United States to arrange sale of such facilities would encounter
difficulties exceeding those met in disposing of United States
surplus property.
Hence, the United States has been prepared to
waive its first-charge principle as applied against cash proceeds
which might possibly accrue from the sale of industrial facilities.
Paragraph 4 j of the Conclusions above states that no claimant
country should be permitted to sell industrial facilities, title
to which has been transferred to it
on reparations account, for
monetary consiaeration.
From this prohibition are excepted sales
by a claimant government to purchasers who would use reparations
industrial facilities within that country's own territories or
jurisciction and sales to its
own
nationals, regardless
of where
(except Japan) its nationals intend to use such assets.
Thus,
the door is opened to the Philippines Government, for example, to
accept an industrial plant for installation and operation by a
Filipino in China; or sale by the Chinese Government of an indus-
trial plant to
an
overseas
Chinese
in Batavia for installation
there.
It would not open the door
to sale by the Chinese to a
Dutchman, Brazilian or Panamanian
unless
such a foreign national
was willing to accept a minority interest, through investments or
capital, in an asset wich remained legally the property of the
original claimant country or one of its nationals.
The language
of this policy decision is sufficiently flexible to allow claimant
countries to realize certain valucs from assets which
they would
not, with their own personnel and resources alone, be able to
utilize profitably.
It is believed at the same time that the
restriction implied in the policy statement would curtail dumping
of industrial facilities upon Pacific markets in such a way as to
depress prices for new equipment exoorted on commercial account
in the normal processes of trade, as might be the case if
unrestricted resale for
cash were frankly permitted.

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.99)
S E C R E T
12. Paragraph 4 k of the Conclusions provides for a cut-off
cate when transfer and delivery of reparations assets must have
been completed. The Supreme Commander has indicated the view
that that date should be two years subsequent to actual designa-
tion of facilities title to which will be transferred to repara-
tions claimants. The present view of the U.S. Government is that
completion of the reparations program should be accomplished if
at all possible by January 1, 1948, but that th formulation of
necessary inter-Allicd policy decisions may delay implementation
of those decisions and render impractical a cut-off date earlier
than July 1, 1948, or possibly even January 1, 1949. If at the
finally determined cut-off date, facilities which have been
selected and allotted to a claimant country, remain in Japan due
to the failure of that country to accept title and provide trans-
portation for its removal, control over the facility should revert
to the Supreme Commander for such disposal as may be determined
in other cirectives.

.SECRET
REPARATIONS REMOVALS OF INDUSTRIAL FACILITIES
AND ME.CHANT SHIPPING FROM JAPAN
Memorandum by the Army Member, SWNCd
7 Apr11 1947
1. As a result of the outcome of recent discussions
between representatives of the State, War and Navy Departments
and representatives of the Pauley and Strike reparations groups,
agreement, subject to SWNCC approval, has been reached on a U.S.
position for a comprehensive Japanese reparations removals
program.
2.
These discussions culminating months of effort on the
part of the U.S., both unilaterally between Washington and
General MacArthur, and on a governmental level in the FEC, have
resulted in agreed conolusions which should be set into motion
as soon as practical.
3. Although an effort should be made to secure FEC agree-
ment, indications are that agreement is improbable within a
reasonable period of time and that final agreement will only come
with the signing of the peace treaty. Inasmuch as the prompt
handling of the Japanese reparations problem is essential in the
attainment of U.s. occupational objectives and in alleviating
Japanese economic and industrial inertia, thereby reducing U.S.
costs, it is believed that prompt unilateral U.S. action will be
necessary.
4. A second paper on the implementation of removals of in-
dustrial facilities and merchant shipping from Japan has been
drafted and will be presented to SWNCC in the near future.
5. A third paper on the control of the economic war potential
of Japan has also been drafted and will be presented to SWNCC in
the near future. This paper will be a substitute proposal for
SWNCC-302, now under consideration in the FEC (FEC-O84 Series).
6. It is requested that the enolosure be approved by SWNCC
aa a matter of urgenoy

FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. At the direction of the Assistant Secretary of War a
conference was held in New York on 3l Maroh and 1 April between
representatives of the State, War, and Navy Departments and
representatives of the Pauley and Strike Groups. Agreement was
reached on a U.S. position for a comprehensive Japanese repara-
tions removals program.
2. This agreement, contained in the Conolusions, was

Sir:
894.60/4-847
On April 3, the United States Government instructed
General Douglas MacArthur to transfer some of the Japanese
VR
assets to the four Allied Powers as interim reparations.
Under this directive, China will receive fifteen percent of
the available industrial facilities; the Philippines five
percent; the Netherlands five percent, for the Indies; and
AN
the United Kingdom five percent, for Burma, Malaya and its
595.60
colonial possessions in the Far East.
Last year Ambassador Edwin W. Pauley, the President's
Reparations Commissioner, surveyed economic conditions in
Korea and Japan, and recommended that part of the industrial
CS/A
equipment in Japan be transferred to Korea. However, Korea
was left out of the advanced reparations for emergency relief
purposes.
As you doubtlessly know, many factories in Korea are idle
because of the lack of equipment and spare parts for machinery.
Consequently,
The Honorable
DIVISION OF
NORTHEAST ASIAN AFFAIRS
The Secretary of State
MAY 5- 1947
C
4
in
Washington, D. C.
C
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
9 1947

=== Page 534 === (confidence 0.99)
2
Consequently, unemployment has been steadily increasing and
inflation has become alarmingly acute.
The economic disintegration is causing unrest in Korea
and making the American task difficult. Hence it would seem
wise to carry out Ambassador Pauley's recommendation which
would help to avert imminent economic disaster in Korea. If
some of the machinery tools, iron and steel in Japan were made
available to Korea at this time when the need is most urgent,
it would speed her economic recovery and reduce the economic
drain on the United States.
Furthermore, Korea has suffered the longest from Japanese
political oppression and economic exploitation, which is directly
responsible for Korea's present plight. Therefore it is only just
that Korea be given her share in all future and final reparations
settlements with Japan.
Your kind consideration of the above will be greatly ap-
preciated.
Respectfully yours,
Yongjeung Kim
Piesident
Korean Affairs Institute
C.C.:The Honorable Edwin W. Pauley

My dear Mr. Kim:
Your letter to the Secretary of April 8, 1947 has been referred
to me for reply.
As you point out, the United States Government has directed
General MacArthur to transfer to China, the Philippines, the Nether-
lands for the Netherlands East Indies, the United Kingdom for Burma,
Malaya and UK colonial possessions in the Far East certain percentages
of Japanese industrial facilities available as reparations.
These transfers represent advance reparations transfers for
emergency relief purposes. They are being made to countries which suf-
fered war devastation in the Pacific theater. Unfortunately, Korea
cannot be included among those countries eligible for advance repara-
tions. Technically, Korea was not an Allied belligerent in World
War II and it suffered only a small amount of direct war devastation.
The United States Government is fully cognizant of the present
economic difficulties of South Korea and of the extent to which this is
due to years of Japanese economic exploitation and political oppression
of Korea. This factor will most certainly be taken into account in any
disposition of the eventual U.S. share of Japanese reparations. The
United States Government has already expressed its intent, as you know,
to assign to Korea a part of its own share of those Japanese industrial
facilities in Japan which are made available to it under its repara-
tions claim. In addition, it is the position of the United States Gov-
ernment that all industrial facilities in Korea which were formerly
owned by the Japanese should be exclused from the Japanese reparations
settlement and should remain the property of the Korean people.
Sincerely yours,
For the Secretary of State;
Edwvin mlmantin

Division of Japanese and Korean

DIRECTIVE TO THE SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
INTERIM DIRECTIVE REGARDING ADVANCE TRANSFERS OF JAPANESE
REPARATIONS
AND REPARATIONS
ALLOCATION PROCEDURES
FOR
INDUSTRIAL FACILITIES
IN
JAPAN
The following Interim Directive, Serial Number 75, received
from the State, War, and Navy Departments, issued under the
provisions of paragraph III, 3, of the Terms of Reference of the
Far Eastern Commission, is furnished for your guidance.
PART A
1. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Fowers should effect
immediate delivery of assets within Japan in accordance with the
provisions of the Directive, Serial Number 69, February 19, 1947,
regarding the delivery of reparations goods in Japan, as advance
payment on account of ultimate reparations shares providing:
a. That such assets fall within categories of Japanese
facilities and equipment which have been properly declared as
available for reparations transfer by the Far Eastern Com-
mission and designated for removal by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers;
b. That such assets should be declared by the Supreme
Commander for tho Allied Powers to China, the Philippines,
the Netherlands (for Netherlands East Indies) and the United
Kingdom for Malaya, Burma and its colonial possessions in the
Far East;
C. That such assets should be delivered to a recipient
country only after it has supplied evidence acceptable to the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, or to some other
agent or agency hereafter designated to evaluate and pass
judgment on such evidence, that the
(1) Immediate and useful employment of such.assets is
practicable, and

=== Page 540 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
(2) Employment of rehabilitated, repaired or recon-
structed facilities can contribute, directly, to immediat
relief and rehabilitation requirements of the war-devastat
economy of the claimant country or, indirectly, to the
relief needs of other parts of Asia devastated by Japanese
armed forces;
d. That no country listed in subparagraph b should receive
delivery of more than 5%, excepting China which may receive
15%, in quantity or value of any single category of assets.
available as determined in subparagraph a above, and author-
ized for transfer under the provisions of subparagraph c
above.
e. That assets specified for delivery should represent in
their total quantity or value, not more than 3o% of any single
category of available assets as determined in subparagraph a
above.
2. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers should designate
for delivery on advance transfer account such a balanced selection
of plants and facilities as will not represent disproportional
reduction of value or quality of over-all residual industrial
capacities available for later transfer as reparations.
3. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers should maintain
such records as necessary to enable offset for any such advance
transfers to be made against ultimate fulfillment of approved
and recognized over-all national reparations shares.
PART B
1. The following procedures should govern SCAP implementation
of those aspects of the Japanese reparations program providing for
the removal from Japan of industrial facilities and equipment.

STANDARD PORM NO. 64
GENERAL HILLDRING
TO
: A-H - General Hilldring
FROM : JK - Mr. Whitman
sUBJECT: Financing Self-Liquidating Projects for Japan.
A number of weeks ago this division submitted a draft cable on the
financing of self-liquidating projects, addressed to SCAP. Concurrence
was reached with the War Department and there was an understanding that
b
the cable would go shortly after (one week) there had been dispatched a
cable bearing on the general subject of restoring trade.
Colonel Gilchrist later suggested that the State Department not
press for the dispatch of the financing cable until after SCAP had replied
to the cable covering restoration of trade generally. Upon receipt of that
suggestion, JK ceased to press for the already-agreed-to cable on financing.
0
The subject of self-liquidating ("link") projects has been advanced at
the Inter-Allied Trade Board as a means whereby nationals of this country
and those of other countries represented on the FEC would participate in
the financing of raw materials into Japan. The general attitude of the
Board is that the U.S. should provide equality of opportunity and this
would best be assured through formal announcement by the U.S. member, together
with all the information which can be provided and which would enable other
nationals to judge the possibilities of such an approach in order for Japan
to obtain raw materials. JK feels there is much merit in the Board position.
In order, therefore, to avoid possible charges that the U.S. is acting
unilaterally in this matter, JK would like to provide formal notification
of the self-liquidating ("link") projects and formally invite other countries
to participate. It is felt that the dispatch of the cable and SCAP's
answer would provide a basis for the contemplated formal action and we
therefore request A-H to reconsider with Colonel Gilchrist the immediate
dispatch of the subject cable.
PN Ph tran
DK:DOBowman:bcb
4/10/47
1098

=== Page 548 === (confidence 0.99)
NDARD FORM NO. 64
TO
SWNCC
DATE:Apr11 16, 1947
FROM : General Hilldring
SUBJECT:
The State Department opposes the adoption of SWNCC 236/44.
With the exception of one clause in Part II, paragraph llc,
the entire contents of this paper have either been approved
previously by the SWNCC or included by the State Department in
instructions to the U.S. delegate on the Far Eastern Commission
as derivative in substance from approved SWNCC policies or as
representing matters of solely political interest, not properly
of concern to SWNCC.
Of this latter character is Part I, paragraph 4, proposing
percentage distributions of Japanese industrial assets among the
claimant countries, which was approved by Secretary Marshall and
sent to the U.S. delegate on the FEC on April 4, 1947.
The remainder of the paper provides a manual for implementa-
tion by SCAP of the reparations program. Such a manual, repre-
senting a compilation from approved SWNCC policy papers, acted
upon by FEC or issued by the U.S. as interim directives, is a
highly desirable project. It is not clear, however, why SWNCC
should be called on to repeat its previous actions in approving
these statements. It is even less clear why the FEC should be
4-1647
confused by being asked to take action in this form on policies
which they have already approved, or are now discussing.
Part II, paragraph llc contains a clausepermitting SCAP to
retain facilities, otherwise to be removed as reparations, to
meet the needs of the occupation. The State Department believes
that this provision was inadvertently omitted from SWNCC 236/43
and proposes that that document be amended by the insertion of
the following provision as paragraph 3c of SWNCC 236/43:
"In addition to the facilities to remain in Japan,
as provided in this document, SCAP may retain temporarily
any items deemed essential to the needs of the occupation."

To:
SWNCC - Mr. Moseley
From:
Subject:
Reparations Removals ofIndustrial Facilities from Japan.
Attached hereto is a compilation which it is proposed shall be dispatohed
by the War Department as Executive for the Joint Chiefs of Staff to SCAP. The
purpose of this statement is to arrange in a convenient form and to clarify the
application of means for implementing the reparations removals of industrial
facilities and merchant shipping from Japan. It is believed that the statement
embodies all pertinent official policy decisions pertaining to procedures for
the selection, allocation and delivery of reparations goods in Japan. It has
been compiled by a working committee of the State, War and Navy Departments from
official SWNCC and FEC papers. Because of partial treatment of this subject in
several previous papers it has been considered desirable to give SCaP a single
document which contains all of the official latest decisions.
A few minor modifications from existing SWNCC papers have been made as
noted below.
a. Material not previously approved in SWNcC is contained in
paragraph 6o (other disposition), 7 c (time limit). However, in
H
each of the two cited cases, working level agreement has been secured.
b. Material previously approved by SWNCC but not previously
transmitted to SCAP in JCS directive is included in paragraphg (accounting
6
and delivery).
4
c. Modification in form for clarity or completeness in previous
language have been made in paragraph 4 b (accounting and delivery),
6 c (time limits), 7 a (representation) and 9 a (miscellaneous -
re-written).
It is requested that you review the attached statement for correctness
as to quotations from SWNCC papers and for consistency (where paraphrasing
or new language is involved) with SWNcc policy and advise this office of your
findings.
Attachment.

Disposition of Industrial Facilities
1. Selection
a. In acoordance with provisions of directives to SCAP regarding availability
for reparations transfer of categories of Japanese industry, SCAP should be responsible
for final seleotion of those partioular industrial facilities and equipment to be
made available for removal from Japan as reparations (para 1 a SWNCC 236/45; WAR 95604).
b. In making above seleotions SCAP should give consideration to the follow-
ing::
(1) Security and industrial disarmament requirements:
Facilities which have been employed in primary and secondary
war industries and faoilities in war-supporting industries,
the operation of which was direotly and closely related to
war industries, should be removed in preference to those
not so employed.
(2) The achievement of a fair balance between:
(a) The general preferences of reparations claimants for
plants, machinery, equipment and other facilities,
1. of modern and efficient design and manufacture,
2.
in good working condition and capable of being
removed from Japan with minimum loss of value and
-2-

=== Page 558 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
and efficienoy,
3.
in consolidated or integrated units,
of special value or need to claimant countries,
and
(b) The legitimate needs of Japan's peacetime economy for
similar equipment, having due regard:
1. for the geographical location of individual
plants in reference to markets, raw materials,
manpower, fuel supply, and complementary facilities;
2。
for variations in specific products as among
types, sizes and other variable characteristics;
3. for the feasibility of repair and rehabilitation
in Japan.
(3) The occupation policies of dissolving large industrial
and banking corporations which have exercised control over
a great part of Japanese trade and industry (para 1, 2, 3,
FEC 091/4).
o. Insofar as ownership is a basis for seleotion, highest priority
should be given to the removal of plants belonging to Zaibatsu concerns and lowest
priority to removal of plants in which nationals of members of the United Nations
have substantial interest (para 4 j. SFE 162/15).
a. Inventory and Evaluation.
a. SCAP should be responsible for preparing an inventory of individual
Japanese industrial facilities selected for reparations removals. This inventory
shall include any available information which would aid a prospeotive claimant in
determining the utility of the individual facility, such as technical specifications,
make, age, and condition (para 1 b, SWNCC 236/45; WAR 95604).
-3-

=== Page 559 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
b. Scap should be responsible for assigning monetary valuation to industrial
facilities selected for reparations removals. Procedures for uniform monetary
valuation should be determined by ScsP. Valuations should be in sufficient detail
to permit charges to be made for parts of plants which may be allocated separately.
Values assigned to reparations assets need have no relation to any other values so
long as these values are consistent among the reparations assets comprised of
industrial facilities and equipment (para 1 o SWNCC 236/45; WAR 95604).
c. SCAP should submit to representatives of countries, members of the FEC,
the inventory provided in a and the valuation provided in b above (para 1 d swycc
236/45; WAR 95604).
d Upon receipt of the inventories and valuations mentioned above, a
country, member of FEC may lodge with SCAP olaims for the allocation to it of parti-
oular industrial facilities (para 1 e SWNCC 236/45; WAR 95604).
& Allocation
a From those items of industrial facilities and equipment declared avail-
for reparations removal
able/SCAP should allocate partioular industrial facilities to each country on the
basis of olaims submitted by that country.
b. In making allocations to claimant countries SCAp should, to the extent
practicable, give consideration to striking a fair and equitable balance as between
all claimant countries of items:
(1) in integrated or consolidated units
(2) of modern and efficient design and manufacture
(3) in good working condition.
o. To the extent that reparations claims of two or more countries are
in conflict SCAp will work out what seems to him to be the most reasonable allocation
taking into primary consideration the proportion of each country's allocation still
unfilled. After taking into account this primary consideration, consideration should

=== Page 560 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
be given the following four itams in stated order of priority:
(1) olaims for whole plants and related facilities, as opposed
to olaims for portions theroof.
(2) claims: for parts of plants or related groups of items which
can be integrated with the existing industrial pattern of
the claimant country.
(3) the extent to which the olaimed item or items would replace
property which was destroyed, demaged, or looted in the war,
or requires. replacement because of excessive wear inoident
to prosecuting the war against Japan.
(4) the extent to which the claimant country depended in pre-
war years on imports: from Japan of the item or items (or
produots thereof) claimed (para g., h., SWNCC 236/45; WAR 95604).
4. Accounting and Delivery.
a. Upon receipt of directives indicating the overall shares assigned to each
country, SCAP should set up appropriate reparations accounts for recipient country.
b. The value of the facilities received by any one country should not
exceed its percentage share of the total value of the industrial facilities melected
and valued by SCAP for removal as reparations. At the time an allocation is made,
SCAp will make an initial charge of the monetary value assigned to the item or items
(in accordance with 2 b above) to the reparations account of the recipiant country.
When the final title is taken by a recipient country to an individual faoility aboard
ship or other appropriate craft, a final charge shall be made against the reparations
account of the recipient country, taking into account, if necessary, any unforeseen
loss of value (para 1 f, SWNCC 236/45; WAR 95604).
c. The port should be designated by SCAP after consultation with the
claimant country.
CONPIOINTTAL
-5

=== Page 561 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
d. In delivering reparations goods in Japan to claimant countries, the
Japanese Government will bear all costs connected with dismantling, packing, trans-
porting to the port in Japan for waterborne craft or for airborne craft, handling,
and loading on board craft at that port. None of the above-mentioned costs will be
paid by the reoipient country; however, all these costs involved in the delivery of
reparations goods should in the future be appropriately charged toward the repara-
tions accounts of the recipient countries in the same proportions as their respeotive
national shares of reparations from Japan. The charging of these costs to the
reparations accounts of recipient oountries should not affect the distribution of
reparations received by claimant countries.
 Before proceeding with the removal of designated goods from their
location, SCAP will require the recipient country to provide him with a written
undertaking to accept such goods, provided they are delivered on board at the
designated port free from any major damage sustained in processes of dismantling.
packing, transporting, handling, and loading. SCap will likewise agree to reserve
the accepted facility for the olaimant country. The Japanese Government will be
required to make good any damage or shortage resulting from the processes of dis-
mantling, packing, transporting, handling, and loading if such damage or shortage
occurred prior to delivery on board the carrying ocraft.
f. The goods in question would become the property of the recipient
country when they have been delivered on board at the designated port and thereafter
they become the entire responsibility of such recipient country. Receipt for the
goods shall be given by the recipient country when they have been delivered on
board the carrying oraft at the designated port.
g. Each recipient country will be responsible for arranging for the
waterborne or airborne craft necessary to transport its reparations goods from
the designated port in Japan. For purposes of developing priorities and sohedules

T. Representation of Claimant Countries in Japan.
a Each country represented on the FEC should be permitted to have a

arising ove5 claims for partioular facilities (para 1 j SWNCC 236/45; WAR 95604).
8. Resolution of Disagreements
In event of disagreement between SCap and a member country concerning the
allocation of a particular facility by SCAP, if formal protest is made to SCAP by
two-thirds of the members of RTAC, SCAP, who has final authority in such matters,
may, in his disoretion, refer the dispute to the U.S. Government (para 1 j SWNCC
236/45; WAR 95604).

I agree that, until this question is disposed of in the peace treaty, the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers should be allowed to retain some
fishing and whaling facilities. However, it is the State Department's
view that the general amendment to SWNCc 236/43 which would permit the
Supreme Commander to retain temporarily any facilities deemed essential
to the needs of tho ocoupation, adequetely covers this point without
pre-judging the peace treaty. It is understood however that retention
of an item or facility deemed essential to the needs of the occupation
does not relieve the Supreme Commander of the obligation to make the
item available for claim and therefore for possible ultimate ramoval.
I agree that the language with regard to the light metals industry
needs revision in the interest of clarity. Since there is no dismgreement
as to the substance of such an amendment, I suggest that the Department
of State instruct the U.S. Member of the Far Eastern Commission as to
appropriate language to be substituted for the present provision. In
this cornection, however, I am not prepared to accept the proviso: "that
at no future time any of the non-ferrous motal rolling capaoity will be
mnde available for claim, except insofar as this recommendetion is modi-
fied by SCAP in accordance vith the provisions of paragraph 1 b." This
proviso is not in accord with intent of Svaic0 236/45 and is both con-
fusing and inconsistent.
I suggest that the proposed letter to General Macarthur be revised
to meet the objections which I have stated. Moreover, I think it would
be appropriate to advise him that we must be prepared to make some
concessions in order to secure the approval of at least a majority of
the Far Eastern Com ission countries to this program. In this connection,
I call your attention to the following statement made by the State Depart-
ment representative at the New York meeting of the SWNCc-Strike-Pauley
conferees:
"As a whole, the paper represents a reasonable solution whioh I
am prepared to recommend that the Department of State accept.
"The decisions with respect to ball-bearings and primery aluminum
production are, in my judguent, subject to the most question. However,
in relation to the entire paper the differences between the agreed pro-
posals and the original Pauley recommendations do not seem of suffioient
importanoe to justify a formel reservation.
*It 1s

FROM : ESP - Mr. Martin
SUBJECT: Draft Cable f6F SCAP.
The attached draft cable to SCAP is necessary, in our view, because
of the difficulties which we are likely to encounter in negotiating in
FEC the proposal in the U.S. final reparations program on electric fur-
nace steel capacity. In the interim reparations program the FEC last
June approved reduction of electric furnace capacity to 100,000 m.t.
annually. In December, SCaP was given authorization to retain an addi-
tional 300,000 m.t. of capacity until 30 June 1947, when the authoriza-
tion will expire unless SCAP requests and the FEC approves an extension.
If, as we believe, SCAP desires to have the authorization extended, it
is urgent that his request be transmitted to the FEC well in advance of
30 June.
If the FEC adopts the proposal in the final program, overall steel
ingot capacity would be reduced to 3, 500,000 m.t., as in the interim
program, but SCAP would be left full discretion in determining the a-
mount of this capacity which should be in electric furnace facilities.
Because this proposal involves a definite departure from the policy
approved in the interim program, we anticipate difficulty in obtaining
FEC approval unless we can supply information as to the amount of electric
furnace capacity which, in the judgment of SCAP, should be retained under
the final program. We have also not been able to make a satisfactory
reply to questions raised in FEC as to why SCAP has designated for removal
cnly about 173,000 m.t. of electric furnace capacity out of about
1,800,000 m.t. which should apparently be available under the interim
program. We are, therefore, asking the aid of SCAP in supplying in-
formation which will facilitate FEC approval of U.S. policy on electric
furnace capacity in Japan.
Will you please undertake to clear the attached cable with A-H, and
if they conour, follow through with the War Dept. as a matter of urgency?
Will yon pleaae follow through wit the War Lleapt.
as a maltr  urgency?

The Honorable George C. Marshall
Secretary of State
Washington, D. C.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
My dear Mr. Secretary:
The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission provide
that one of the functions of the Commission should be to "formulate the
policies, principles and standards in conformity with which the fulfill-
ment by Japan of its obligations under the Terms of Surrender may be
accomplished."
It is further provided that when such decisions are made by the
Far Eastern Commission, "The United States Government shall prepare
directives in accordance with the policy decisions of the Commission and
894.60/5-2247
shall transmit them to the Supreme Commander through the appropriate
United States Government agency."
At the fifty-eighth meeting of the Far Eastern Commission held
at 2516 Massachusetts Avenue, Northwest, Washington, D. C., on 22 May 1947,
the enclosed policy decision relative to The Selection of Plants for Repa-
rations Removals, Destruction or Retention in Japan was approved.
As Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I have been
instructed to forward this decision to you on behalf of the Commission,
in order that the appropriate directives may be prepared and transmitted
to the Supreme Commander in accordance with the Terms of Reference.
In adopting the enclosed policy decision the Commission agreed
that it should be released to the press after being received by the
Supreme Commander. In accordance with cur normal procedure I would
A/MK894
appreciate it if you could make arrangements to notify me when the en-
closed policy decision has been received in Tokyo.
Sincerely yours,
Nelom Iuelu
JUN 22 1947
Nelson T. Johnson
FILED
Secretary General

J. H. Hilldring
Assistant Secretary of State
894.60/6-1947
Subject:
Program of Strike Corporation in Japan
On 19 June you sent a message to SCAP on this subject which
contained the following paragraph:
"Part 5. WD intends to make every effort to
secure full US govtal auth to proceed with final
reparations program on same unilateral basis as
advance delivery directive. atter to be discussed
with Secy State tomorrow. Consider independent
certified report on Japanese industry is needed to
provide maximum unbiased factual support for uS
unilateral action respecting not only Japanese
reparations and economic recovery programs but also
for peace treaty provisions favorable to our avowed
intention to assist in world economic stabilization
and prevention of political unrest. State Dept
fully shares this view."
It is not clear to me what is meant by the statement that the
report of the Strike Corporation will provide additional factual
support for a Japanese economic recovery program and for the
Japanese peace treaty provisions. It had been the understanding
CS/
of the Department that the Strike Gorporation was authorized to
check the specific plant selections made by SCAP within the
framework of FEC policy decisions with specific regard to the
reparations program and the retention of essential facilities in
Japan. It would be appreciated if you would clarify what further
functions the Strike Corporation report is expected to serve.
I understand, of course, that a clear definition of reparations
894.
WAS
y12
SECRET

=== Page 591 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
removals will facilitate Japanese economic recovery and the deter-
mination of the United States policy in the formulation of a peace
treaty with Japan. Nevertheless, I shouad like to know whether
you envision the Strike Corporation report as extending to any
subject other than reparations removals and permanent plant re-
tentions. The State Department, of course, feels that we should
not wait for the Strike Corporation to report before proceeding
forthwith with both the Japanese recovery program and the pre-
paration of the United States position on a peace treaty.
I am, thus, disturbed by the concluding sentence of the above-
quoted portion of your message to SCAP indicating full State Depart-
ment endorsement of the program outlined by you.
The Department could make no such commitment until it more
fully understands the implications of the language used.
The message in question further implies that the State Department
would support unilateral action by the United States on (1) Japanese
reparations, (2) Japanese economic recovery, and (3) peace treaty
provisions. Such a position is obviously inconsistent with our
engagements to participate fully in the operations of the Far Eastern
Commission. The State Department does not, in any sense, contemplate
unilateral action by this Government in these broad fields.
I would strongly suggest that SCAP be informed that the State
Department had not seen or anproved this program. As a preferable
alternative, I would urge that you and I meet to discuss this matter
and agree on a more precise description of the assignment of the
Strike Engineering Corporation for communication to General MacArthur
as a corrected version of Part 5 of your message. Such an amended
version could then bear, without qualification, the endorsements of
both the State and War Departments.
In the meantime, it would be appreciated if you would send me,
et your earliest convenience, e copy of the contract with the Strike
Engineering Corporation so that we might be informed as to its specific
terms.
J. H. Hilldring
Assistant Secretary

In sending forward Mr. Martin's memorandum attached
hereto concerning Mr. Peterson's message to General
MacArthur regarding reparations and future U.S. policy,
I should like to call attention to another aspect of the
matter not mentioned by Mr. Martin. In the paragraph
quoted in the atta ched memorandum it is stated that the U.s.
Government should proceed with the final reparations pro-
6
gram "on same unilateral basis as advance delivery directive.
1947
In the "Survey of the Attitudes of the States Composing
the Far Eastern Commission" forwarded by General MoCoy
in his letter of June 20, 1947, the statement 1e made:
"In the future, if the U.S. should attempt to settle
by interim directives all the remaining reparations
problems as well as the economic levels of Japanese
industry, it would probably be resented by all of
the other states on the Commission even, perhaps, to
the extent of causing the break-up of the Far Eastern
Commission."
It is believed that in addition to the considerations
raised by Mr. Martin serious thought should be given by
the officers of the Department to the effect on our
international relations particularly in FEC and in making
8
preparations for the Japanese peace conference of any action
along the lines advocated by Mr. Peterson.

From:

Department of State
Subject: Program of Strike Corporation in Japan.
On 19 June you sent a message to SCAP on this sub-
ject which contained the following paragraph:
"Part 5. WD intends to make every effort
to secure full US govtal auth to proceed with
final reparations program on same unilateral
basis as edvence delivery directive. Matter
to be discussed with Secy State tomorrow. Con-
sider independent certified report on Japanese
industry is needed to provide maximum unbiased
factual support for US unilateral action respect-
ing not only Japanese reparations and economic
recovery programs but also for peace treaty pro-
visions favorable to our avowed intention to
assist in world economic stabilization end pre-
vention of political unrest. State Dept fully
shares this view."
I do not know what assignment you propose for the
Strike Corporation which would provide additional factual
support for the Japanese reparations program, for a
Japanese economic recovery program, or for Japanese peace
treaty provisions. I had not understood that the purpose
of their trip to Japan was related to eny of these matters.
Rather, I had assumed that they were to check the specific
plant selectlons made by sCAP within the framework of FEC
policy decisions with respect to reparations removals and
with respect to the retention of facilities in Japan.

=== Page 595 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
I am concerned about the implication that the work
of the Corporation is essential to an economic recovery
program and to the peace treaty. I do not like the idea
of having to hold up either of them for the six months
their job is supposed to take.
I am, therefore, disturbed by the concluding sentence
indicating full State Department endoreement of this pro-
gram.
I am even more disturbed by the implication that the
State Department has endorsed unilateral action by the
United States with respect to the settlement of the
Japanese reparations problem and with resnect to a Japanese
economic reccvery prcgram. As you know, this is not the
case.
I urge that, at the minimum, SCAP be informed that
the State Department had not seen or approved this para-
graph. It would be better still if you and I could agree
on a more precise statement of the assignment of the Strike
Engineering Corporation which could be communicated to
General VacArthur as a corrected version of this paragraph,
endorsed without reservation by State and War.
J. H. Hilldring
Assistant Secretary
